VERS. 22.-- « Viro cujus. » Ecce cum quaereretur quare misero data est lux, ostendit hic, via abscondita est viro: quia si jam bene agit, quid aget in fine nescit; vel aliquando quae putantur Deo placere, displicent.

763AVERS. 23.-- « Circumdedit eum Deus. » (GREG.) Quia licet coelestia desideret, tamen quid de se intus dispositum sit nescit. Sunt et circa eum tenebrae ignorantiae, praeteriti est immemor, futura non invenit, praesentia vix novit, intima dispensationis Dei penetrare nequit. Qui vero has tenebras intendit, flet: et omni visu supernam lucem requirit: unde fit aliquando, ut in pio fletu interni gaudii claritas erumpat; unde sequitur:

VERS. 24.-- « Antequam comedam. » Comedere est contemplationis luce pasci, quam non habet nisi qui prius in hoc exsilio, quasi ubi non sunt bona gemit, et suspirat desiderio et exsequitur vim lacrymarum.

« Et quasi inundantes aquae, etc. » Aquae inundantes multis voluminibus impetu feruntur, sic in 763Blugentibus multa volumina, dum qui apud Deum decernatur, nesciunt: dum praeterita sua recolunt, futura pertimescunt, et in bonis suis ne errent, verentur: quos dum flagella Dei corripiunt, se offendisse suspicantur: unde addit:

VERS. 25.-- « Quia timor. » Flent enim, quia deseri se timent: et ne pia percussio non sit disciplinae, sed vindictae, unde: « Quis novit potestatem irae tuae? (Psal. LXXXIX.) » Aliquando enim est gratia quam dicimus iram, et aliquando ira quam dicimus gratiam, unde necesse est ut in omnibus timeatur; in prosperis, ut non sint sibi data ad majorem excaecationem, non ad consolationem, ut bonis solet fieri, ne sit in adversis initium poenae.

VERS. 26.-- « Dissimulavi. » (Ibid.) Et si semper 763Ccogitando, etc., usque ad si Job qualis in potestate fuit ad exemplum proponit.

« Nonne dissimulavi? » Potestas habita et ad utilitatem cogitanda, et propter tumorem dissimulanda, et potens ut prodesse debeat, posse se sciat.

« Nonne silui? » Ecce qualis est in ore.

« Nonne quievi? » Ecce qualis erga illicita, etc., usque ad quia nostra perfectio culpa non caret, unde addit:

« Et venit super me indignatio. » Unde magna arte dicturus verbera, praemisit recte facta, ut in his quisque consideret quae poena maneat iniquos, si ita hic castigat justos, ut si justus vix salvabitur, impius ubi parebit?

CHAPTER III.

  • 759B

VERS. 1.-- « After these things, etc. » He makes them attentive, as it were to hidden things which were covered in closed vessels, but which are uncovered when their mouth is opened, that we may be refreshed by their inmost fragrance. Thus far a threefold exposition; from here on, according as the several passages require.

(GREG.) « He cursed his day. » Behold, this is not according to the surface of the history, because this is both evil and impossible. For the day on which he was born no longer was, and it is idle to curse a thing that does not exist, but pernicious if it did exist; nor can a past day now be turned into darkness, nor can the other things that follow suit it, nor would the untimely birth (which he says afterwards) enjoy rest: therefore here nothing is according to the letter.

759C« After these things. » That is, after all the aforesaid things which he suffered, his friends also being silent. Since therefore he patiently gave thanks in all things and taught well, he is not now, with none instigating him, to be believed to break out into cursing from impatience; but he says this calmly, because amid evils he praised God. « He cursed. » (Ibid.) A curse is spoken of in two ways, etc., as far as and applies the remedy.

VERS. 3.-- « Let the day perish on which I was born. » Not created, etc., as far as but brought in the darkness of sin.

« Let the day perish. » The day is the prosperity of the world, which ends in night, because it leads on to tribulation; or the day is the delight of sin, the night the blindness of the mind. Man is spoken of in three ways: by nature, by fault, and 759Dby weakness. Man therefore is born in the day, conceived in the night: because he is not carried off to the delight of sin unless he is first weakened through the darkness of the mind. But let the day perish, that is, let the delight of sin be destroyed by the vigour of justice.

« And the night. » (Greg. in Job l. IV.) That is, let penitence extinguish what the blinded mind perpetrates through consent, while it does not warily see through the blandishments of delight, lest the fault which begins to flatter should drag it to destruction.

VERS. 4.-- « That day, » etc. (Ibid.) That is, let it be seen, at the outset of delight, to what end of perdition it hurries a man, and let it be tormented by penitence. But if it has been so punished. « Let it not require. » In judgment, that he may punish; 760Aand he will not illumine. He illumines with light who reproves; but he is as it were covered who is not recalled into the memory of the judge. Whence: « Whose sins are covered (Psal. XXXI), » nor then let them be shown in the sight of all.

VERS. 5.-- « Let it not be illumined, etc., let them darken it. » That is, the day of delight, lest it be seen by him who sees all things. « Darkness. » (GREG.) That is, the laments of penitence, or the hidden judgments of God, from which we are absolved by prevenient grace, which we know not how to merit. « And the shadow of death. » That is, the death of Christ according to the flesh, etc., as far as in what mercy has succoured him, let man punish it. Whence follows: « Let gloom seize upon it. » That is, the confusion of the mind, of which it is said: « For there is a confusion that brings glory. (Eccli. IV.) » « And let it be wrapped about. » On every side. « With bitterness. » 760BOf penitence. But if the day, that is, the delight of sin, is so punished, what of the night, that is, the consent to fault?

VERS. 6.-- « Let a dark whirlwind possess that night. » It is as it were a whirlwind of a storm stirred up, etc., as far as for the blindness of the damnation in which we were born. « Let it not be counted in the day of the year. » The year is the grace from above, etc., as far as but is forever without share in that fatherland. Whence he adds:

VERS. 7.-- « Let that night be solitary. » Because it is separated from that throng, or loses the man whom it had made its companion; and when the elect have been redeemed by grace, he alone with his own body is handed over to Gehenna. « Nor worthy of praise. » Man, when laid low, worshipped idols, in which he praised demons as the darkness of night; 760Cbut now that the idols have been rejected, the night is worthy of praise.

VERS. 8.-- « Let them curse it. » (Ibid.) In the old translation, etc., as far as and at the end, being loosed, may openly wage wars. « Leviathan. » That is, their addition, that is, of men, in whom after his suggestion he does not cease to add evils. Or a reproach, because when he promised to add divinity to Adam, he took away even what he had. He is called by the prophet a twisting serpent, who softly, as by the coaxing endings of words, pours in his snares. And a bar, because being hard through malice he strikes unto death. Once roused he will act openly, but meanwhile in secret.

VERS. 9.-- « With gloom » Of that night. « Let the stars be darkened. » That is, hypocrites, who shine by their 760Dworks before the eyes of men. These are they which are drawn by the tail of the dragon, that is, by the last persecution of Antichrist; these are darkened, because the malice of the enemy so prevails over them that they are wrapped in open evils, such outwardly as they are within. « Let it look for light. » This according to his members, etc., as far as which by prophesying waited for Christ, but did not know him when he came. « Nor the rising of the dawn as it springs up. » (Ibid.) The rising of the dawn is the new birth of the resurrection, in which the saints shall rise with their flesh to see the eternal light. But however much the elect shine here, they cannot penetrate what that glory of that new birth will be. This night did not close but opened the doors of the womb, because, when man was conceived, it unbarred the desires of concupiscence unto sin. 761AAnd when these doors, that is, the desires of carnal concupiscence, have been unbarred, we are dragged on to innumerable evils of corruption. Whence, weighed down, we groan; because justice requires this, that what we did willingly we should endure unwillingly.

VERS. 10.-- « Because he did not close the doors of the womb, etc. » This of the head himself, etc., as far as as we say that robbers give life to their captives, if they do not take it away.

VERS. 11.-- « Why did I not die in the womb. » It is not to be believed, etc., as far as lest suggestion should draw him to delight. « And having come forth from the womb. » (GREG.) Because sin conceived outwardly, etc., as far as while they bear the strife of their own flesh against the spirit.

VERS. 13, 14.-- « For now. » (Ibid.) By "now," 761Bwhich belongs to present time, he designates the rest that stands always in the present, that is, eternal rest. « I should rest with kings. » That is, with the angels, in that they rule the things subject to them. Who are also consuls, because by announcing to us the will of God they take counsel for us. Or by kings he means holy preachers, who rule themselves; and consuls of the earth, because they counsel earthly men well. He says therefore that if he had not sinned, he would have ascended thither even unredeemed, whither the apostles and holy preachers after the redemption arrive with great labour, who are not deferred as the ancients were, but soon after death enter heaven. « Who build, » Every earthly man is pressed in his heart by dense tumults of thoughts, and is trampled by the throng of desires as by crowds. But to build solitudes is to drive out from the secret place of the heart the tumults of earthly desires, 761Cand to pant after the love of inmost rest, so that it may be said: One thing I have asked of the Lord, and this will I seek after, etc.

VERS. 15.-- « With princes who possess gold. » The princes are the rulers of the Church, etc., as far as but he who has foreseen things to come already calls to mind things past.

VERS. 16.-- « Or as an untimely birth. » (Ibid.) Because that which is an untimely birth comes forth before its time, and being extinguished is straightway hidden away. These are the saints, with whom he considers that he could have rested, who from the beginning of the world were before the time of the redemption, and yet mortified themselves to the world. The first time, before the law, which brought forth fathers dead a second time, was as it were the womb of an untimely birth; and they were as it were dead from the womb, 761Dwho without the tables of the law feared God by nature, and believing in the Redeemer to come slew their pleasures. « Hidden. » Because, few being recorded by Moses, etc., as far as whom the spirit of prophecy irradiated, as Job himself. (Ibid.) Now he opens the rest of this light, and shows in plainer words what is daily done in it concerning the manner of life of the wicked.

VERS. 17.-- « There the wicked. » That is, in the light of Christ, which the ancients awaited, the Gentiles cease from the throng of the desires of the world, and with tranquil mind already foretaste eternal rest. And this is: « And there they rested. » In the world the strong are said to be strong in vigour, not wearied. But he who is strengthened in the love of God healthfully 762Afails from his own strength; and the more strongly he seeks eternal things, the more wearied he is, and dies to earthly things. Whence: « My soul longeth and fainteth for the courts of the Lord (Psal. XXXVIII). » When by "there" he means the light, he shows the place of the elect in which they are contained.

VERS. 18.-- « And they who once were bound. » The just, though at rest from desires, yet while in the body are bound by the trouble of their own corruption. For the body weighs down the soul, and the flesh strives against the spirit. There are also bodily chains, hunger, thirst, and the like, which here cannot be loosed; but from them all they shall be loosed in the liberty of the glory of the sons of God. See the degrees: the wicked, being converted, cease; the well-exercised rest in the inmost bosom; those absolved from the chains of corruption come to liberty. But what they did here meanwhile, he adds: « Who have not heard, 762Betc. « The exactor is the devil, who once bestowed on man the coin of deception and daily demands the debt of death; his voice is temptation. He hears who is tempted and resists; he hearkens who consents. The same concerning inward peace, because what the mind greatly loves it repeats the oftener.

VERS. 19.--Small and great are there. » (Ibid.) Because here there is a distinction of works, there it will be of dignities. Whence: « In my Father's house there are many mansions. (Joan. XIV.) » « And the servant. » He who sins is the servant of sin, because here he never becomes free while he fears the judge; but there he will be free, where there will be no doubt about pardon. There will indeed be there a memory of fault, not such as defiles the mind, but such as, without injury to blessedness, binds one more closely to blessedness and gladness, as when healed 762Cwe remember our pains without pain; whence we give the more thanks to the physician.

VERS. 20.-- « Why is light given to the wretched? » (GREG. in lib. Job lib. V.) Having contemplated that rest, where, made free by the Lord, he looks down upon the prosperities of the world, which draw men back from it. « Why is light given to the wretched, » that is, prosperity in the wretchedness of our pilgrimage: because, although it does not utterly crush the just man, yet it divides the mind between the love of God and the management of itself. Whence he adds: « And life to those who, etc. » There is bitterness to every just man, etc., as far as or to be set over others in rule.

VERS. 21.-- « Who look for death. » (Ibid.) That is, they desire to mortify themselves utterly to the world, but by the hidden judgment of God it does not come, because they are compelled to be taken up with the honors laid upon them, which they bear in the fear of God; 762Dand within is the desire of godliness, and without they fulfil the ministry of their order, lest through pride they gainsay the disposing of God: and thus they both profit many, and, in that they see themselves imperfect, they rise the higher through humility: and in that they are put off from their desires, by that very delay they are enlarged toward the same: whence he adds, as though digging out, etc. « As those digging out a treasure. » Because he who seeks a treasure by digging, etc., as far as he finds examples of contemplation, whereby he may be rich.

VERS. 22.-- « To the man whose. » Behold, whereas it was asked why light is given to the wretched, he shows here: the way is hidden from the man; because, if he now does well, he knows not what he shall do at the last; or sometimes the things that are thought to please God displease him.

763AVERS. 23.-- « God hath compassed him about. » (GREG.) Because, although he desires heavenly things, yet he knows not what has been ordained within concerning himself. And about him also are the darknesses of ignorance: he is unmindful of the past, he does not find out things to come, things present he scarcely knows, the inmost things of God's dispensation he cannot pierce. But he who bends his mind upon these darknesses weeps, and with his whole sight seeks the light above: whence it sometimes comes to pass that in a godly weeping the brightness of inward joy breaks forth; whence there follows:

VERS. 24.-- « Before I eat. » To eat is to be fed with the light of contemplation, which none has save he who first, in this exile, as in a place where good things are not, groans, and sighs with desire, and follows out the force of tears.

« And as overflowing waters, etc. » Overflowing waters are borne with a rush in many rollings; so in 763Bthem that mourn there are many rollings, while they know not who is adjudged before God: while they call to mind their own past things, they greatly fear the things to come, and in their own good things they are afraid lest they err: and while the scourges of God take them to task, they suspect that they have given offense: whence he adds:

VERS. 25.-- « For the fear. » For they weep, because they fear to be forsaken: and lest the godly stroke be not of discipline, but of vengeance, whence: « Who knoweth the power of thy wrath? (Psal. LXXXIX.) » For sometimes there is a grace which we call wrath, and sometimes a wrath which we call grace; whence it is needful that he be feared in all things: in prosperity, lest the things be given him for a greater blinding, not for consolation, as is wont to befall the good; lest in adversity there be the beginning of punishment.

VERS. 26.-- « I have dissembled. » (Ibid.) And if always 763Cby thinking, etc., as far as whether he sets forth Job, such as he was in power, for an example.

« Have I not dissembled? » Power possessed is both to be thought upon for profit, and to be dissembled because of swelling; and he that is powerful, that he may be bound to profit others, should know that he is able.

« Have I not kept silence? » Behold what he is in his mouth.

« Have I not been quiet? » Behold what he is toward things unlawful, etc., as far as because our perfection is not without fault, whence he adds:

« And indignation is come upon me. » Whence, being about to speak of stripes with great art, he set his rightly-done deeds first, that in them every man might consider what punishment awaits the wicked, if thus he here chastises the just; so that, if the just man shall scarcely be saved, where shall the ungodly appear?

CAPUT IV.

763DVERS. 2.-- « Si coeperimus loqui tibi. » (Ibid.) Molliter incipiunt, sed in aspera prorumpunt. Sic haeretici blanda verba proponunt, ne caveantur, sed aspera subinferunt.

« Sed conceptum. » (Ibid.) Perfecti nec in corde perversa concipiunt quae silentio compescunt. Alii prava concipiunt, sed silentii vigore magno constringunt: alii et concipiunt et mox efferunt. In sensu leves, in lingua praecipites. De his est Eliphaz, qui suo experimento de omnibus idem sentit.

« Tenere quis poterit. » Quasi vellet retinere: in quo innuit se in loquendo offensurum.

VERS. 3.-- « Ecce docuisti plurimos. » (Ibid.) Certa bona sunt quae et ille fatetur, qui criminari 764Avult. Quantus ante fuit qui tot suis occupatus disponendis, etiam libere alios docuit, actu et verbo recta ostendens? Quia vero criminator bona de eodem dicit, ad crimen inflexit, dicens:

VERS. 5.-- « Nunc autem venit, etc., quasi non servasti recta quae dicebas. » Nota autem quod sunt iniqui, et bona justorum quae nota negare non possunt, dicunt, ne pravi videantur: et ut inde magis credatur eis, in malis quae inserunt, et haec ipsa bona ad cumulum reatus invertunt. Hic recta Job ergo dixisse, sed non servasse, quasi suo testimonio malus reprehenditur: inferius male loqui notatur, ut taceat. Et hi sunt duo modi quibus bonos impetunt mali, dicentes eos vel prava dicere, vel bona quae dicunt non tenere.

764B« Defecisti, etc. Ubi est timor tuus? » Quasi de virtutibus miretur perditis, quas congruo ordine ponit. Sicut in via saeculi audacia fortitudinem, timor debilitatem parit, ita et in via Dei audacia debilitatem, timor Dei fortitudinem: quia qui Deo jungitur, potestate quadam super omnia levatur; fortitudo non nisi in adversis ostenditur, unde patientia additur, quia in fortitudine quasi proficit, qui mala aliena non motus tolerat. De patientia perfectio nascitur, unde in patientia animam possidere, homo dicitur, id est, in omnibus recte vivere, cunctis motibus mentis ex virtute dominari: qui enim se vincit, contra omnia est fortis.

(Ibid.) Quoniam autem invehendo corripuit, nunc quasi exhortando subdit:

764CVERS. 7.-- « Recordare, obsecro te. » Sed et hoc reprehensibiliter agit. Innocens enim et rectus saepe hic funditus delentur, etsi ad aeterna servantur: sed qui non pro aeterna sed terrena mercede Deo serviunt, ipsi sibi fingunt quod quaerunt, et docere praesumentes cum terrenam securitatem praedicant cunctis suis laboribus ostendunt quod amant.

VERS. 8.-- « Quin potius vidi eos. » (Ibid.) Dolores seminat, qui dicit. Fraudulenta metit, qui dicendo praevalet. Vel dolores seminat, qui perversa agit. Metit, qui in eis prosperatur. Hi non (ut ait) pereunt, sed felicius hic justis vivunt. Patitur enim Deus quosdam hic praevalere, ut mundius vita justorum purgetur. Punit mox alios, ne putetur humana negligere, et multa mala praeterire, ut corda innocentium, 764Dqui pusillanimes sunt, confirmet: verius autem diceret de omnibus, in fine; generaliter poterat dici, quod innocens et rectus non perit, quia etsi hic atteritur, apud Deum vera salute reparatur. Et mali pereunt, quia etsi hic altius crescant, in fine durius ferientur. Sed quia dicit, recordare, patet transacta ad mentem reduci, non futura enuntiari.

VERS. 9.-- « Flante Deo perisse. » (Ibid.) Nos conflamus aerem, dum ab extra intus trahimus, et ab intus extra reddimus. Deus ergo in retributione flare dicitur, quia ab exterioribus causis intus judicii consilium concipit, et ab interno consilio exterius sententiam emittit, id est, a malis nostris quae extra 765Avidet, judicium intus disponit, et ab interno conceptu extra damnationem facit. Vel flare Deus, id est, irasci dicitur per simile, quia nos cum irascimur, flatu furoris inflammamur. Flat ergo Deus irascendo vindictam cogitans, cum, post longam patientiam, qui in se tranquillus est reis videtur turbulentus, cum ipsi coram eo reatu suo turbentur.

(GREG.) Postquam clementer monuit, aperte increpat. « Rugitus leonis, » id est, severitas et terror ipsius Job: « vox leaenae, » loquacitas conjugis, « dentes catulorum, » edacitas filiorum destructa est. Rigidus exsultat, quasi jure essent damnata. Tigridi etiam Job comparat pro maculis simulationis. Hypocrita enim, qui rectus videtur, habet latentia vitia, 765Bquae aliquando erumpentia colorem variant. Praeda est justorum gloria, quam sibi arripit: putans ergo iste sanctum Job bona quae in eo noverat, per hypocrisim tenuisse, dicit:

VERS. 11.-- « Tigris periit. » Hoc est varietas tuae simulationis exstincta est, etc., usque ad hi tales per sapientiam putant se omnes transcendere.

VERS. 12.-- « Porro ad me dictum est verbum absconditum. » (Ibid.) Abscondita se haeretici audire simulant, unde et latenter praedicant, ut sanctior eorum praedicatio habeatur, non aliis aequa.

« Furtive, etc. » (Ibid.) Quia non cum aliis per ostium intrant, sed rimas quaerunt pravae intelligentiae, quia vero fur lumen timet, addit:

VERS. 13.-- « In horrore visionis nocturnae. » 765CIn pavore nocturnae visionis se percepisse venas susurri divini dicunt, quia ut alta jactent, vix ea posse se capere dicunt. Sed dum ita alta notant, testes contra se nequitiae sunt. In nocturna enim visione dubie cernitur.

« Quando solet sopor occupare. » Hoc est, dum omnes inferius dormiunt torpore, nos ad alta et superna percipienda vigilamus. Et dum aliquando hoc despici vident, seipsos timere simulant quod dicunt, unde subdit:

VERS. 14.-- « Pavor tenuit me. » Ut miri videantur in altis, quasi pertimescunt quae fingunt: et tamen audaces sunt proferre, quae asserunt vix se potuisse audire.

« Ad me dictum est verbum. » (Ibid.) Filius Dei 765Ddicitur, etc., usque ad eo magis rectitudinem ejus repressa formidat: unde subdit:

« In horrore visionis nocturnae, etc. » Id est, in pavore occultae contemplationis, in qua nihil manifeste, sed quasi imaginarie, videtur. Sed ad hanc nemo pertingit, nisi carnalibus desideriis repressis, unde:

« Quando solet sopor occupare homines. » Vigilat qui curis saeculi intendit: dormit in quiete qui fugit. Hic est Jacob qui in itinere dormit et angelos videt, id est, in cursu hujus vitae a saecularibus rebus oculos claudit quod ad concupiscendum culpa primis parentibus aperuit. Et nemo ad videnda interna perducitur, nisi ab his quae exterius implicant 766Asubtrahatur; unde: « Vacate et videte: quoniam ego sum Deus (Psal. XLV). » Sed quo animus in contemplatione levatus super se altiora conspicit, eo in se terribilius tremit, unde:

« Pavor tenuit me et tremor et omnia ossa. » (Ibid.) Id est, fortiora acta, etc., usque ad stimulum carnis sentit, addit:

VERS. 16.-- « Stetit quidam. (Ibid.) Ut incomprehensibilia se novisse judicent, etc., usque ad quia bonum commune specialiter sibi arrogavit.

« Inhorruerunt pili carnis meae. » (Ibid.) Pili sunt superflua quaelibet humanae corruptionis cogitationes vitae veteris: quas si a mente excludimus, pilos carne incidimus, ut non dolore fatigemur. Unde Moyses: « Levitae radant omnes pilos carnis suae 766B(Levit. XIV). » Haec autem subtilius in nobis conspicimus, cum alta penetramus, unde recte nunc dicitur:

« Cum spiritus transiret. » Cum enim mens vera videt, durius se de superfluis cruciat. Transeunte ergo spiritu, pili pertimescunt, quia ante vim compunctionis superfluae cogitationes fugiunt, quia intima visio animam contra se inflammat; et dum illicita districte resecat, mens latius speculationi inhaeret, et bene figit spiritum qui transibat, nec tamen haec ipsa mora contemplationis vim pietatis plene aperit, unde subdit:

« Stetit quidam cujus non agnoscebam vultum. » (Ibid.) Quidam non dicitur, etc., usque ad per coaeternam ejus speciem conspicit, subdit:

766C« Imago coram oculis me. » Imago Patris Filius est, qui dicit: Nemo venit ad Patrem, nisi per me.

« Et vocem quasi aurae lenis audivi. » (Ibid.) Vox aurae, cognitio, etc., usque ad qui ex hoc auditu, didicit.

VERS. 17.-- « Nunquid homo comparatione Dei justificabitur? » Hoc in contemplatione novit, quod justitia humana divinae comparata injustitia, ut quae lucerna in tenebris lucet, in sole caligat: quod potuit, quia qui lucem videt, scit quid de tenebris aestimet.

« Aut factore suo purior? » (Ibid.) Hoc aestimat qui contra flagellantem murmurat. Sed debet scire, quia impie non flagellat, qui mira ex nihil fecit, et 766Dconditor naturae non est iniquus judex culpae. Etsi iste injuste Job redarguit, recte tamen modum creaturae in comparatione Creatoris describit. Puriorem ergo se vir factore suo aestimat, si contra flagellum querelam parat, eumque sibi procul dubio postponit, cum ejus judicium de sua afflictione redarguit

« Ecce qui serviunt ei. » (IDEM.) Angelus etsi in statu suo immutabilis permanet, in hoc tamen quod creatura est, vicissitudinem mutabilitatis habet, et ita stabilis non est. Si enim natura haec mutabilis non esset, cum esset bene condita, in nullis cecidisset. Sed ex arbitrio habuit ruere vel stare, et in stantibus meritum fuit, motum mutabilitatis suae, studio voluntatis fixisse. Hoc mutabilitatis argumentum est quod de apostata subdit:

767A« Et in angelis suis reperit pravitatem. » Ex quorum comparatione infirmitas humana colligitur.

« Quanto magis hi qui habitant domos luteas. » (GREG.) Id est terrena corpora, etc., usque ad conspiciunt curis saeculi occupati; unde subdit:

VERS. 20.--De mane usque ad vesperam. » Id est, a vitae suae exordio usque ad terminum perpetratione iniquitatis vulnerantur, a qua succisi in profundum ruunt.

(Ibid.) Vel mane, prosperitas; vespera, adversitas; sed per utrumque pereunt, quia per prospera lascivi, per adversa impatientes ad insaniam surgunt. Et quia nullus eorum, scilicet, « qui de mane usque ad vesperam succidentur » intelligit, quid bonis afflictis hic servetur, quid sibi futurum qui 767Bhic bona putant. Sed quia non omnes sic deseruntur, subdit:

« Qui autem reliqui fuerint. » Id est, despecti mundi, quos saeculum ut indignos reliquit, ut puer Aegyptius, id est, amator saeculi, peccatis niger, saepe a saeculo despectus relinquitur, ut non cum eo currat, sed fractus adversis torpeat. Sed hunc David, id est, Christus, invenit, et in suum amorem convertit, et cibo verbi pascit, et ducem itineris, id est, praedicatorem suum facit: quo duce convivantes, id est, mundi laetitiam destruit, quae eum comitem habere despexit.

« Auferentur ex eis. » Quia Deus sibi eos elegit.

« Morientur, et non in sapientia. » Interposita sorte justorum, redit ad interitum non intelligentium. 767CReprobi justos despiciunt, quia per mortem suam invisibilia quaerunt, vel per mortem vitam invisibilem quaerunt. Sed ipsi, qui pariter mortem et sapientiam fugiunt, sapientiam quidem penitus deserunt, nolentes pro veritate mori, mortem tamen non evadunt.

CHAPTER IV.

763DVERS. 2.-- « If we begin to speak to thee. » (Ibid.) They begin softly, but they break out into harsh things. So heretics set forth smooth words, that they be not guarded against, but bring in harsh ones after.

« But that which is conceived. » (Ibid.) The perfect do not even in their heart conceive perverse things, which they check by silence. Others conceive depraved things, but bind them fast by the great strength of silence: others both conceive them and straightway bring them forth. Light in understanding, headlong in tongue. Of these is Eliphaz, who out of his own experience judges the same of all men.

« Who shall be able to hold it. » As though he would fain hold it back: wherein he hints that he will give offense in speaking.

VERS. 3.-- « Behold, thou hast taught very many. » (Ibid.) There are certain good things which even he confesses who wishes to lay a charge 764Aagainst a man. How great was he before, who, though busied with the ordering of so many things of his own, yet freely taught others also, showing right things by deed and by word? But because the accuser speaks good things of the same man, he bent them to a charge, saying:

VERS. 5.-- « But now it is come, etc., as though thou hast not kept the right things which thou didst say. » But note that there are wicked men who speak the good things of the just which, being known, they cannot deny, lest they seem depraved: and that they may thereby be the more believed in the evils which they bring in, they turn these very good things also to the heaping up of guilt. Here therefore, as though by his own testimony, he is reproved as an evil man for having spoken right things, but not kept them: below he is noted for speaking ill, that he may hold his peace. And these are the two ways in which evil men assail the good, saying that they either speak depraved things, or do not hold the good things which they speak.

764B« Thou hast fainted, etc. Where is thy fear? » As though he marveled at virtues that were lost, which he sets down in a fitting order. As in the way of the world boldness begets strength and fear begets weakness, so also in the way of God boldness begets weakness, and the fear of God strength: because he who is joined to God is raised by a certain power above all things; strength is not shown save in adversities, whence patience is added, because he as it were makes progress in strength who bears others' evils unmoved. Of patience is perfection born, whence a man is said to possess his soul in patience, that is, to live rightly in all things, to have dominion out of virtue over all the motions of the mind: for he who conquers himself is strong against all things.

(Ibid.) But since he took him to task by inveighing against him, now he adds as it were by exhorting:

764CVERS. 7.-- « Remember, I beseech thee. » But this too he does blameworthily. For the innocent and the upright are often here utterly blotted out, even though they are kept for things eternal: but they who serve God not for an eternal but for an earthly wage feign for themselves the thing they seek, and, presuming to teach, when they preach earthly security, show by all their labors what they love.

VERS. 8.-- « Nay rather I have seen them. » (Ibid.) He sows sorrows who speaks. He reaps deceitful things who prevails by speaking. Or, he sows sorrows who does perverse things. He reaps, who prospers in them. These do not (as he says) perish, but live here more happily than the just. For God suffers certain men to prevail here, that the life of the just may be purged the more cleanly. He punishes others straightway, lest he be thought to neglect human things, and to pass over many evils, that he may strengthen the hearts of the innocent 764Dwho are faint-hearted: but he would speak more truly of all men at the end; it could be said generally that the innocent and the upright man does not perish, because though he be worn down here, with God he is restored in true salvation. And the evil perish, because though they grow higher here, at the end they shall be smitten more hardly. But since he says, remember, it is plain that past things are brought back to mind, not that things to come are declared.

VERS. 9.-- « That they perished at the blowing of God. » (Ibid.) We blow air, while from without we draw it within, and from within we render it without. God therefore is said to blow in retribution, because from outward causes he conceives within the counsel of judgment, and from that inward counsel he sends forth the sentence outwardly, that is, from our evils which he sees without, 765Ahe disposes judgment within, and from the inward conception he makes damnation without. Or God is said to blow, that is, to be angry, by a likeness, because we, when we are angry, are inflamed with the blast of fury. God therefore blows in being angry, purposing vengeance, when, after long patience, he who in himself is tranquil seems turbulent to the guilty, since they before him are troubled by their own guilt.

(GREG.) After he had admonished him kindly, he openly rebukes him. « The roaring of the lion, » that is, the severity and terror of Job himself: « the voice of the lioness, » the talkativeness of his wife, « the teeth of the whelps, » the greediness of his sons, has been destroyed. The stiff man exults, as though they had been condemned by right. He compares Job also to a tiger, because of the spots of dissembling. For the hypocrite, who seems upright, has hidden vices, 765Bwhich sometimes, breaking out, change his color. The glory of the just is a prey which he snatches to himself: this man therefore, thinking that holy Job had held by hypocrisy the good things which he knew in him, says:

VERS. 11.-- « The tiger hath perished. » That is, the variety of thy dissembling is put out, etc., as far as such men as these think that they surpass all men in wisdom.

VERS. 12.-- « Moreover a hidden word was spoken to me. » (Ibid.) Heretics feign that they hear hidden things, whence they also preach secretly, that their preaching may be held holier, and not equal to that of others.

« By stealth, etc. » (Ibid.) Because they do not enter with the rest by the door, but seek the chinks of a depraved understanding; and because a thief fears the light, he adds:

VERS. 13.-- « In the horror of a nightly vision. » 765CIn the dread of a nightly vision they say that they have perceived the veins of the divine whisper, because, that they may boast of lofty things, they say that they can scarcely take them in. But while they thus mark lofty things, they are witnesses against themselves of their wickedness. For in a nightly vision a thing is seen doubtfully.

« When deep sleep is wont to seize. » That is, while all below sleep in torpor, we watch for the perceiving of high and heavenly things. And when at times they see this despised, they feign to fear themselves the things they say, whence he adds:

VERS. 14.-- « Dread held me. » That they may seem wonderful in high things, they are as it were greatly afraid of what they feign: and yet they are bold to bring forth the things which they assert they could scarcely hear.

« A word was spoken to me. » (Ibid.) The Son of God 765Dis said, etc., as far as so much the more, being checked, does it dread his uprightness: whence he adds:

« In the horror of a nightly vision, etc. » That is, in the dread of hidden contemplation, in which nothing is seen manifestly, but as it were in an image. But to this no man attains, unless the desires of the flesh be checked, whence:

« When deep sleep is wont to seize men. » He watches who is intent upon the cares of the world: he sleeps in quiet who flees them. This is Jacob, who sleeps on the way and sees angels, that is, in the course of this life he closes his eyes to worldly things, which fault opened to our first parents for coveting. And no man is brought to the seeing of inward things unless he be withdrawn from those things which entangle him without; 766Awhence: « Be still and see: for I am God (Psal. XLV). » But the more the mind, lifted up in contemplation, beholds things higher than itself, the more terribly does it tremble in itself, whence:

« Dread held me and trembling and all the bones. » (Ibid.) That is, the stronger deeds, etc., as far as he feels the sting of the flesh, he adds:

VERS. 16.-- « There stood one. (Ibid.) That they may judge that they have known incomprehensible things, etc., as far as because he arrogated to himself specially a good that is common.

« The hairs of my flesh stood up. » (Ibid.) The hairs are all the superfluous thoughts of human corruption, of the old life: which if we shut out from the mind, we cut the hairs from the flesh, that we be not wearied with pain. Whence Moses: « Let the Levites shave all the hairs of their flesh 766B(Levit. XIV). » But these things we behold more subtly in ourselves when we pierce into high things, whence rightly it is now said:

« When a spirit passed by. » For when the mind sees true things, it torments itself the more hardly concerning superfluous things. When therefore the spirit passes, the hairs are greatly afraid, because before the force of compunction superfluous thoughts flee away, because the inward vision inflames the soul against itself; and while it cuts away unlawful things strictly, the mind cleaves more widely to contemplation, and it well fixes the spirit which was passing; nor yet does this very staying of contemplation fully open the force of loving-kindness, whence he adds:

« There stood one whose countenance I knew not. » (Ibid.) He is not said to be a certain one, etc., as far as he beholds through his co-eternal likeness, he adds:

766C« An image before the eyes me. » [Vulg. Job 4:16 meis, "an image before mine eyes"] The Image of the Father is the Son, who says: No man comes to the Father but by me.

« And I heard a voice as of a gentle breeze. » (Ibid.) The voice of the breeze is knowledge, etc., as far as who from this hearing learned.

VERS. 17.-- « Shall a man be justified in comparison of God? » This he knew in contemplation, that human justice compared with the divine is injustice, as the lamp which shines in the darkness grows dim in the sun: which he could do, because he who sees the light knows what to reckon of the darkness.

« Or purer than his maker? » (Ibid.) This he reckons who murmurs against him that scourges him. But he ought to know that he does not scourge impiously who made wonders out of nothing, and that the 766Dfounder of nature is not an unjust judge of fault. And though this man unjustly reproves Job, yet he rightly describes the measure of the creature in comparison with the Creator. A man therefore reckons himself purer than his maker if he prepares a complaint against the scourge, and without doubt sets him after himself, when he reproves his judgment concerning his own affliction

« Behold, they that serve him. » (IDEM.) An angel, although he remains unchangeable in his estate, yet in this, that he is a creature, has the vicissitude of changeableness, and so is not stable. For if this nature were not changeable, since it was well founded, it would have fallen in nothing. But of free choice it had it to fall or to stand, and in those that stood the merit was that they fixed the motion of their own changeableness by the earnestness of the will. This is the proof of changeableness which he adds concerning the apostate:

767A« And in his angels he found wickedness. » From the comparison of these the infirmity of man is gathered.

« How much more they that dwell in houses of clay. » (GREG.) That is, earthly bodies, etc., as far as they behold, being taken up with the cares of the world; whence he adds:

VERS. 20.--From morning until evening. » That is, from the beginning of their life until its end they are wounded by the committing of iniquity, and being cut down by it they fall into the deep.

(Ibid.) Or morning is prosperity; evening, adversity; but by both they perish, because through prosperity they are wanton, through adversity impatient, and rise up to madness. And because none of them, namely, « who from morning until evening shall be cut down » understands what is kept here for the afflicted good, what is to come for himself who 767Bhere thinks them good things. But because not all are so forsaken, he adds:

« But they that shall be left. » That is, the despised of the world, whom the age has left as unworthy: as the Egyptian boy, that is, the lover of the age, black with sins, is often left despised by the age, that he may not run with it, but, broken by adversities, may lie torpid. But him David, that is, Christ, finds, and turns to his own love, and feeds with the food of the word, and makes the guide of his journey, that is, his preacher: by which guide he destroys them that feast together, that is, the gladness of the world, which scorned to have him for a companion.

« They shall be taken away from them. » Because God has chosen them for himself.

« They shall die, and not in wisdom. » Having set the lot of the just between, he returns to the destruction of them that understand not. 767CThe reprobate despise the just, because through their own death they seek things invisible, or through death they seek the invisible life. But they themselves, who flee alike from death and from wisdom, do indeed utterly forsake wisdom, being unwilling to die for the truth, and yet they do not escape death.

CAPUT V.

VERS. 1.-- « Voca ergo. » (Ibid.) Quoniam hoc veraciter contra iniquos protulit, quamvis latenter Job in numero eorum proposuerit: fastu sapientiae inflatus apertam irrisionem in Job subdit: « Voca ergo, » id est, si afflictus clames, Deus non respondet, id est non dat effectum, quia in tranquillitate eum contempsisti; et « ad aliquem sanctorum, » id est, 767Dsanctos in afflictione adjutores non habes, quia socios in hilaritate non habuisti.

(Ibid.) Post irrisionem subdit sententiam, etc., usque ad haec sapientes turbat, sed ira per vitium stultos trucidat.

« Et parvulum occidit. » Id est, dum ei invidet, quem putat majorem, etc., usque ad vel partem habendo quantitatem nobis restringit.

(GREG. lib. VI Moral., cap. V.) Quia totis desideriis in terreno amore solidatur, unde primus Cain in terra civitatem construxit, alienus a coelo hic felicius degit.

VERS. 3.-- « Et maledixi pulchritudini ejus. » (IDEM.) Quia cum ex visa prosperitate malorum, pedes infirmorum nutent: fortes, videntes quae poena 768Aad gloriam eorum sequatur, prosperitatem eorum reprobant statim. Infirmus cum qualitate rerum mutatur, dum videt prospera, laudat, dum mutantur, nihil esse judicat: sed firmus cum ipsa gloria poenam considerat sequentem, et mox damnat. Sed quia proficientes in mundo multos secum trahunt, subdit:

VERS. 4.-- « Longe fient filii ejus. » Id est, sequaces.

« In porta. » (Ibid.) Porta regni est dies judicii, in quo electi intrant ad gloriam, ante quem filii hujus mundi elati sunt, sed tunc conterentur.

VERS. 5.-- « Et non erit qui eruat. » (Ibid.) Quia illos ibi veritas eripit, quos hic per disciplinam premit.

768B« Cujus messem. » Habet quisque donum intelligentiae, quo recta intelligit et dicit: Sed quia eadem non amat nec facit, a messe quam habet, jejunat; sed qui esurit justitiam ab illo audiens recta, se reficit, unde: « Quae dicunt, facite (Matth. XXIII). »

« Et ipsum rapiet armatus. » (Ibid.) Diabolus ut inermis vincitur, cum aperta mala suggerens, omnia bona similiter tollere conatur: sed armatus vincit, alia bona intacta praeterit, alia latenter corrumpit.

« Et bibent. » (IDEM.) Stultus saepe habet divitias, etc., usque ad et studiosus tarditate praepeditur? respondet:

VERS. 6.-- « Nihil in terra fit sine causa. » Quia inde ille justius de negligentia punitur, iste de studio coronatur. Sed quia ad intelligendum recta 768Cnon modo studio, sed et persecutionibus erudimur, additum: « Et de humo non egredietur. »

« Et de humo non egredietur. » (Ibid.) Humus, id est, elementa mundi in poenam vertuntur delinquentium; unde dicitur: « Pugnabit pro eo, » id est, pro Domino, orbis terrarum contra insensatos, sed tamen de eis non egredietur dolor, quia poena non nascitur de his quae feriunt, sed de his qui peccando percussiones extorserunt: sed quo exterior poena castigat, eo mens ad superna levatur; unde subdit:

VERS. 7.-- « Homo ad laborem nascitur. » Quia impossibile est in hac peregrinatione sine labore esse: sed in eo quod caro affligitur, mens ad petenda alta levatur. « Vel homo, » id est, carnalis, mala 768Dmundi sentit, quia inhianter bona ejus appetit. Sed cujus mens haec transit, ut avis ad alta, penna spei levatus, nil mali sentit, congaudeat et de flagellis, sed quia haec nullus suis viribus valet, subdit:

VERS. 8.-- « Quamobrem ego te. » Id est, illum rogo per quem haec tribui scio.

VERS. 9.-- « Mirabilia absque numero. » Quia divinorum operum magnitudo, nec ex qualitate valet discuti nec ex quantitate numerari.

VERS. 10.-- « Qui dat pluviam. » (Ibid.) Haec verba mystica sunt, quia amici Job societate ejus eruditi erant. « Dat pluviam terrae: » cum arida gentilium corda gratia praedicationis infundit.

« Irrigat autem aquis universa. » Cum hominem 769Aspiritu replet ad fructificandum. Homo dicitur omnia, quia cum omnibus habet aliquid commune. Vel universa, id est, divites et pauperes, et omnes differentias hominum, quia bonum opus sequitur retributio.

VERS. 11.-- « Qui ponit humiles in sublime. » (GREG.) Ut modo despecti judicent cum Domino.

« Moerentes. » Quia quanto magis nunc adversis tribulantur, et se per lamenta castigant, tanto sublimiorem sospitatem habebunt etiam in hac vita. Haec de electis possunt intelligi, ubi humiles alta mente omnia temporalia transcendunt, et afflicti sunt erecti sospitate, non insania, ut qui laetantur cum malefecerint. Hi vero de certitudine aeternae salutis hilarescunt.

769BVERS. 12.-- « Qui dissipat cogitationes. » (IDEM.) Cum reprobi cogitent mala, Deus aliquando ne contra bonos praevaleant, vires tollit, ut effectu careant operis, et apud Deum conscientia rei sint.

VERS. 13.-- « Qui apprehendit sapientes. » (Ibid.) Quia consiliis Dei humana facta etiam tunc congrue serviunt cum resistunt, ut in venditione Joseph, ne adoraretur; in petitione praeputiorum a Saul, ut David exstingueretur; in morte Domini, ne omnes crederent in eum. Deus alia concedit propitius, alia permittit iratus: quae tunc in usum sui consilii vertit, ut quae ejus consilio repugnant, ejus consilio militent; et quod sine ejus voluntate geritur, ejus voluntati contrarium non sit, unde: « Omnia quaecunque voluit, etc. »

769CVERS. 14.-- « Per diem incurrent. » Quia in ipsa veritatis praesentia, perfidiae errore caecati sunt, videntes miracula Christi et de ejus deitate dubitantes.

« Quasi in nocte. » Palpando quaerimus quae oculis non videmus: Judaei miracula viderant, et adhuc quasi palpantes quaerebant: « Si tu es Christus, dic nobis palam (Joan. X). » Sed horum manibus diu retineri non potuit Christus.

« Per diem incurrent tenebras. » (Ibid.) Mens invidi cum de bono alieno affligitur, de radio solis obscuratur.

« Et quasi in nocte sic palpabunt in meridie. » Dies boni operis in proximo exterius lucet: quasi in nocte palpant, quia, livore caeci, aliqua quae reprehendant, 769Dinvenire satagunt. Ecce Loth, id est, justus malis adversantibus intra domum recluditur et munitur, id est ad mentem revertitur et manet interius, Sodomitae caeci circumeunt, id est, invidentes facta dictaque scrutantur, nec ostium, id est, aditum reprehensionis, inveniunt, sed parietem palpant, quia ubique laudabilis actio justi eis obviat.

« Palpabunt. » Sed pauper spiritu, id est, qui apud se non est elatus: salvatur a gladio oris, id est, iniqua persuasione.

VERS. 15.-- « Porro salvum faciet egenum a gladio oris. » Gladius oris est accusatio Judaeorum. Manus violenti, gentilitas quae crucifixit: sed hos Christus resurgendo superavit. In quo egeno, id est, humili populo fidelium spes vitae sequentis solidatur. 770AIniquitas autem, quae modo dilatat os suum in contumeliam Christi, claudet per supplicium, vel conversa cessat ab injuriis. Eadem de pravis generaliter.

« De manu violenti pauperem. » Id est adversa potestate, ut nec blandimentis suasionum trahatur, nec dolore suppliciorum frangatur. Pauper autem non trahitur, quia spes ejus fixa in aeternis contra haec solidatur. Ad cujus spei fructum cum pauper venerit, elatus obmutescit, et contrahit os: quia tormenta linguam ligant, quae loquebatur contra justos detrahendo. Sed ut electus supplicia evadat, et ad praemia pertingat, debet hic flagellis purgari, unde subdit:

VERS. 17.-- « Increpationem ergo Domini ne reprobes. » (Ibid.) Reprobat qui se injuste pati conqueritur. 770BEliphaz ergo putans Job pro purgatione percuti, non probatione fortitudinis, quia Job libere loquitur, causas suae percussionis quaerens, putat eum increpationem Domini reprobare. Sic haeretici quod recte ab Ecclesia agitur, ad malum convertunt.

VERS. 18.-- « Quia ipse vulnerat. » (IDEM.) Duobus modis vulnerat Deus quos ad salutem reducit. Aliquando percutit in corpore ut mentem sanet; aliquando sine exterioribus flagellis mentis duritiam suo desiderio percutit, et sic eam sanat, quae secura in mundo mortua quiescebat. Sed, dum mens ad Deum anhelat et omnia saecularia despicit, omnia mox ei vertuntur in tentationem, et eam affligunt; sed, dum transitorio labore atteritur, a perpetuo 770Cliberatur, et ideo subdit:

VERS. 19.-- « In sex tribulationibus. » (Ibid.) Senario, quem septimus sequitur, praesentis vitae operatio et discursus signatur: quia, sexto die Deus omnia perficiens, hominem condidit, et septima quievit, qui non habet vesperam, id est, finem; in septima ergo tribulatione liberat, ne in septima malum attingat: quia hic paterne erudiens atterit, sed in die judicii a verbere abscondit.

VERS. 20.-- « In fame. » (Ibid.) Enumerat mala praesentis vitae et adjutoria Dei. Fames mentis est silentium verbi Dei: quo subtracto, tentatio carnis invalescit, quae est bellum. Sed Deus etiam in fame a morte animam eripit, quia verbo suo reficit. « Et in bello. » Contra tentationes carnis fortes facit. Sed 770Dtalis factus aliquando detractiones metuit, unde addit:

VERS. 21.-- « A flagello linguae. » Id est, ab exprobratione contumeliae, propter quam irrisus a bono cessat; unde David: « Et a verbo aspero. » Ab hac sanctus absconditur, quia, dum hic laudem non quaerit, nec contumelias sentit; sed, quia post haec aliquis cruciatus corporum timet, sequitur: « Et non timebis calamitatem. » Id est, persecutionis: sed, quia talis in terrore judicii futuri securitatem sibi parat, subdit:

VERS. 22.-- « In vastitate et fame. » Vastitas e s gehenna quae foris, etc., usque ad ut in articulo mortis ejus impetus non horreat, subditur: « Et 771Abestias terrae. » Quae tempore mortis ad rapiendas animas effrenantur, sed saevientes non timet, qui blandientes secutus non est: et initium retributionis est, in obitu securitas mentis. Cum autem bene vivitur, cavendum est ne quis despectis caeteris de singularitate glorietur, sed sociale bonum recogitet, unde addit:

VERS. 23.-- « Sed cum lapidibus regionum pactum. » Quasi divisae sunt regiones gentium, ecclesiae moribus et linguas distantes; lapides electi, qui in aedificio ponuntur. Qui ergo recte vivit pacto se lapidibus jungit: quia in eo quod mundum deserit, sanctis per imitationem se alligat; unde impugnatio crescit, qua tamen attritus humilius Deo subditur, unde subjicit: « Et bestiae terrae. » Non 771Bpacatae sed pacificae, quia non pacem habent, sed pacem faciunt; quia cum acrius homo a diabolo impugnatur, amplius ad amorem impellitur, et cum eo robustior pax efficitur. Vel bestiae sunt motus carnis, quae continentia quasi cavea pressae, etsi tentando rugiunt, non tamen ad effectum quasi ad rabiem morsus excedunt, in quo sunt pacificae. Vel quia perturbant, amplius illud, ubi nulla contradictione vivitur, diligitur: et sic ad amorem intimae quietis impellunt.

VERS. 24.-- « Et scies quod pacem. » (GREG.) Pax vel inchoata, quae hic est concepta ex desiderio Dei; vel plena, quae in futuro ex visione Dei. Cum ergo mens Deo vel caro menti subjugatur, tabernaculum justi, id est, corpus quod mens inhabitat, pacem 771Chabet motibus desideriorum sub justitia refrenatis. Sed nihil est haec castitas carnis, si non adsit mentis suavitas in amore proximi.

« Et visitans speciem tuam non peccabis. » (Ibid.) Species nostra est proximus, etc., usque ad quia ex semine boni operis uberes fructus praedicationis colligit; unde subditur:

VERS. 25.-- « Scies quoque quoniam multiplex erit semen tuum. » Ille enim bene loquendi facundiam accipit, qui bene vivit, nec conscientia loquentem praepedit. Hinc est quod Aegyptii servi facti, frumenta ad semen percipiunt, qui ante liberi ad esum acceperant: quia cum plene Deo subdimur, verbo praedicationis replemur. Ante liberi sacro eloquio pasti; et tamen ad quaedam quae in mundo 771Dpetimus vacantes. Ex praedicatione, magna proles fidelium sequitur. Et hoc est: « Et progenies tua, quasi herba terrae. » Id est, innumerabilis. Vel herbae comparatur, quia, arentem mundi gloriam deserens, spe interna virescit. Justo autem non sufficit haec magna exterius agere, nisi etiam per contemplationem in aeterna penetret; unde subditur:

VERS. 26.-- « Ingredieris in abundantia sepulcrum. (Ibid.) Sepulcrum est contemplatio, etc., usque ad quod utilitati proximorum refundant. « Sicut infertur acervus tritici. » Quia tempus actionis primum est, extremum contemplationis. Et necesse est ut perfectus mentem prius virtutibus exerceat, et eam postea in horreum quietis condat. Vel sepulcrum 772Aest quies aeterna, in qua tanto verius quiescitur, quanto perfectius vita corruptionis necatur. Hanc in abundantia ingreditur, qui post congesta hujus vitae opera, huic mutabilitati plene mortuus, in secreto veri luminis occultatur, sicut triticum: quia justi in alieno tempore percussiones sentiunt, sed in suo, ut grana, in horreum intrant.

VERS. 27.-- « Investigavimus. » In hoc notat se, non secundum superficiem dixisse supra sepulcrum, bestias, lapides, tabernaculum, et hujusmodi, sed in his interiora quaesisse. « Quod auditum mente pertracta. » Verba jactantiae; et si bene docuit, imperitia est velle docere meliorem: unde dicta amicorum etsi recta, non recte dicta arguuntur.

CHAPTER V.

VERS. 1.-- « Call therefore. » (Ibid.) Since he had truly brought this forth against the wicked, though he had secretly set Job in their number, being puffed up with the pride of wisdom he adds an open mockery against Job: « Call therefore, » that is, if in affliction thou criest out, God does not answer, that is, he does not give effect, because in tranquillity thou didst despise him; and « to some one of the saints, » that is, 767Dthou hast not the saints for helpers in affliction, because thou hadst them not for companions in mirth.

(Ibid.) After the mockery he adds a sentence, etc., as far as these things trouble the wise, but anger through vice butchers the foolish.

« And it slayeth the little one. » That is, while he envies him whom he thinks the greater, etc., as far as or by having a part it straitens the quantity for us.

(GREG. lib. VI Moral., cap. V.) Because he is made solid with all his desires in earthly love, whence Cain first built a city on the earth, a stranger to heaven he lives here more happily.

VERS. 3.-- « And I cursed his beauty. » (IDEM.) Because, whereas from the seen prosperity of the evil the feet of the weak totter, the strong, seeing what punishment 768Afollows upon their glory, straightway reprove their prosperity. The weak man is changed with the quality of things: while he sees prosperous things, he praises; while they are changed, he judges them to be nothing: but the firm man, together with that very glory, considers the punishment that follows, and straightway condemns it. But because they that thrive in the world draw many with them, he adds:

VERS. 4.-- « His sons shall be made far off. » That is, his followers.

« In the gate. » (Ibid.) The gate of the kingdom is the day of judgment, in which the elect enter into glory, before which the sons of this world are lifted up, but then they shall be crushed.

VERS. 5.-- « And there shall be none to deliver. » (Ibid.) Because the truth snatches away there those whom here it presses down through discipline.

768B« Whose harvest. » Every man has a gift of understanding, whereby he understands right things and speaks them: but because he neither loves nor does the same, he fasts from the harvest which he has; but he who hungers for justice, hearing right things from him, refreshes himself, whence: « What they say, do ye (Matth. XXIII). »

« And the armed man shall seize him. » (Ibid.) The devil is overcome as unarmed when, suggesting open evils, he tries to take away all good things alike: but he conquers armed, when he passes by some good things untouched and secretly corrupts others.

« And they shall drink. » (IDEM.) The fool often has riches, etc., as far as and the diligent man is hindered by slowness? He answers:

VERS. 6.-- « Nothing on earth is done without cause. » Because thereby the one is more justly punished for negligence, the other crowned for diligence. But because we are schooled to the understanding of right things 768Cnot only by diligence, but also by persecutions, it is added: « And out of the ground it shall not come forth. »

« And out of the ground it shall not come forth. » (Ibid.) The ground, that is, the elements of the world, are turned into the punishment of them that offend; whence it is said: « It shall fight for him, » that is, for the Lord, the world of the earth against the senseless; but yet sorrow shall not come forth out of them, because punishment is not born of those things which strike, but of those men who by sinning have wrung out the strokes: but the more the outward punishment chastens, the more is the mind lifted to things above; whence he adds:

VERS. 7.-- « Man is born to labor. » Because it is impossible in this pilgrimage to be without labor: but in that the flesh is afflicted, the mind is lifted to the seeking of high things. « Or man, » that is, the carnal man, feels the evils 768Dof the world, because he greedily desires its good things. But he whose mind passes beyond these, lifted like a bird to the heights on the wing of hope, feels no evil, and rejoices even in scourges; but because no man avails for these things by his own strength, he adds:

VERS. 8.-- « Wherefore I thee. » That is, I ask him through whom I know that these things are given.

VERS. 9.-- « Wonderful things without number. » Because the greatness of the divine works can neither be examined by quality nor numbered by quantity.

VERS. 10.-- « Who giveth rain. » (Ibid.) These words are mystical, because Job's friends had been schooled by his fellowship. « He giveth rain to the earth: » when he pours the grace of preaching into the dry hearts of the Gentiles.

« But he watereth all things with waters. » When he fills a man 769Awith the Spirit unto fruitfulness. Man is called all things, because he has something in common with all things. Or all things, that is, rich and poor, and all the differences of men, because a recompense follows a good work.

VERS. 11.-- « Who setteth the humble on high. » (GREG.) That they who are now despised may judge with the Lord.

« Them that mourn. » Because the more they are now troubled by adversities, and chasten themselves by lamentations, so much the loftier soundness shall they have even in this life. These things may be understood of the elect, where the humble transcend with lofty mind all temporal things, and the afflicted are raised up in soundness, not in madness, as they who rejoice when they have done evil. But these are glad out of the certainty of eternal salvation.

769BVERS. 12.-- « Who scattereth the thoughts. » (IDEM.) When the reprobate think evil things, God sometimes, lest they prevail against the good, takes away their strength, that they may lack the effect of the work, and be guilty in conscience before God.

VERS. 13.-- « Who catcheth the wise. » (Ibid.) Because human deeds serve the counsels of God fittingly even then when they resist: as in the selling of Joseph, that he might not be adored; in the demanding of the foreskins by Saul, that David might be destroyed; in the death of the Lord, that all might not believe in him. God grants some things being favorable, permits others being angry: which things he then turns to the use of his own counsel, so that the things which fight against his counsel serve his counsel; and what is done without his will is not contrary to his will, whence: « All things whatsoever he would, etc. »

769CVERS. 14.-- « By day they shall run into. » Because in the very presence of the truth they were blinded by the error of unbelief, seeing the miracles of Christ and doubting of his godhead.

« As in the night. » By groping we seek the things we do not see with the eyes: the Jews had seen the miracles, and still as it were groping they were seeking: « If thou art the Christ, tell us plainly (Joan. X). » But Christ could not long be held back by their hands.

« By day they shall run into darkness. » (Ibid.) The mind of the envious, when it is afflicted at another's good, is darkened by the ray of the sun.

« And as in the night so shall they grope at noonday. » The day of a good work shines outwardly in one's neighbor: they grope as in the night, because, blind with spite, they busy themselves to find 769Dsomething to reprove. Behold Lot, that is, the just man, is shut in and fortified within the house while evil men set themselves against him, that is, he returns to his mind and remains within; the blind men of Sodom go about, that is, the envious search out his deeds and words, and find no door, that is, no approach for reproof, but grope the wall, because everywhere the praiseworthy action of the just man meets them.

« They shall grope. » But the poor in spirit, that is, he who is not lifted up in his own eyes, is saved from the sword of the mouth, that is, from wicked persuasion.

VERS. 15.-- « But he shall save the needy from the sword of the mouth. » The sword of the mouth is the accusation of the Jews. The hand of the violent is the heathen world which crucified him: but these Christ overcame by rising again. In which needy one, that is, in the humble people of the faithful, the hope of the life to come is made solid. 770ABut iniquity, which now opens wide its mouth in reproach of Christ, shall close it through punishment, or being converted ceases from injuries. The same things generally of the depraved.

« The poor from the hand of the violent. » That is, from the adverse power, so that he be neither drawn by the blandishments of persuasions nor broken by the pain of torments. But the poor man is not drawn away, because his hope, fixed in things eternal, is made solid against these things. And when the poor man has come to the fruit of that hope, the proud man is struck dumb and draws in his mouth: because torments bind the tongue which was speaking against the just by detraction. But that the elect may escape torments and attain to rewards, he must here be purged by scourges, whence he adds:

VERS. 17.-- « Reject not therefore the rebuke of the Lord. » (Ibid.) He rejects it who complains that he suffers unjustly. 770BEliphaz therefore, thinking that Job was smitten for purgation, not for the proving of his strength, because Job speaks freely, seeking the causes of his smiting, thinks that he rejects the rebuke of the Lord. So heretics turn to evil what is rightly done by the Church.

VERS. 18.-- « For he woundeth. » (IDEM.) In two ways God wounds those whom he brings back to salvation. Sometimes he strikes in the body that he may heal the mind; sometimes without outward scourges he strikes the hardness of the mind with desire of himself, and so heals it, which lay secure and dead in the world. But while the mind pants after God and despises all worldly things, all things are straightway turned for it into temptation, and afflict it; but while it is worn down by a passing labor, it is delivered from the everlasting one; 770Cand therefore he adds:

VERS. 19.-- « In six tribulations. » (Ibid.) By the number six, which a seventh follows, the working and the course of the present life is signified: because on the sixth day God, finishing all things, made man, and on the seventh he rested, which has no evening, that is, no end; in the seventh tribulation therefore he delivers, lest in the seventh evil touch him: because here, chastening as a father, he wears down, but in the day of judgment he hides from the stroke.

VERS. 20.-- « In famine. » (Ibid.) He reckons up the evils of the present life and the helps of God. The famine of the mind is the silence of the word of God: which being withdrawn, the temptation of the flesh grows strong, which is war. But God even in famine snatches the soul from death, because he refreshes it with his word. « And in war. » Against the temptations of the flesh he makes men strong. But 770Dsuch a one sometimes fears detractions, whence he adds:

VERS. 21.-- « From the scourge of the tongue. » That is, from the upbraiding of reproach, for which, being mocked, he ceases from good; whence David: « And from the sharp word. » From this the holy man is hidden, because, while he seeks not praise here, neither does he feel reproaches; but because after these things a man fears some torment of bodies, there follows: « And thou shalt not fear calamity. » That is, of persecution: but because such a one prepares security for himself in the terror of the judgment to come, he adds:

VERS. 22.-- « In wasting and in famine. » The wasting is e s the gehenna which is without, etc., as far as that in the moment of death he may not shudder at its onset, there is added: « And 771Athe beasts of the earth. » Which at the time of death are let loose to snatch away souls, but he does not fear them raging who has not followed them fawning: and the beginning of the recompense is, at death, security of mind. But when a man lives well, heed must be taken lest any one, despising the rest, glory in his singularity, but let him consider the good of fellowship, whence he adds:

VERS. 23.-- « But with the stones of the regions a covenant. » As it were: the regions of the nations are divided, churches differing in customs and tongues; the stones are the elect, who are set in the building. He therefore who lives rightly joins himself by covenant to the stones: because in that he forsakes the world, he binds himself to the saints by imitation; whence assault grows, by which nevertheless, being worn down, he is more humbly made subject to God, whence he adds: « And the beasts of the earth. » Not 771Bpacified but peacemaking, because they do not have peace, but make peace; because when a man is more sharply assaulted by the devil, he is the more driven to love, and with him a sturdier peace is wrought. Or the beasts are the motions of the flesh, which, pressed by continence as by a cage, though they roar in tempting, yet do not go so far as the effect, as it were to the madness of biting, wherein they are peacemaking. Or because they disturb, that is the more loved wherein men live with no gainsaying: and thus they drive us to the love of inmost quiet.

VERS. 24.-- « And thou shalt know that peace. » (GREG.) Peace either begun, which is here conceived out of the desire of God; or full, which is in the world to come out of the vision of God. When therefore the mind is made subject to God, or the flesh to the mind, the tabernacle of the just man, that is, the body which the mind inhabits, has peace, 771Cthe motions of the desires being bridled under justice. But this chastity of the flesh is nothing, if there be not present the sweetness of the mind in the love of one's neighbor.

« And visiting thy beauty thou shalt not sin. » (Ibid.) Our beauty is our neighbor, etc., as far as because out of the seed of a good work he gathers the rich fruits of preaching; whence there is added:

VERS. 25.-- « Thou shalt know also that thy seed shall be manifold. » For he receives the eloquence of speaking well who lives well, and whose conscience does not hinder him as he speaks. Hence it is that the Egyptians, being made servants, receive grain for seed, who before, being free, had received it for eating: because when we are fully made subject to God, we are filled with the word of preaching. Before, being free, they were fed with the sacred eloquence; and yet they were at leisure for certain things which we 771Dseek in the world. Out of preaching a great offspring of the faithful follows. And this is: « And thy offspring, as the grass of the earth. » That is, innumerable. Or it is compared to grass, because, forsaking the withering glory of the world, it grows green with inward hope. But it is not enough for the just man to do these great things outwardly, unless he also pierce through contemplation into things eternal; whence there is added:

VERS. 26.-- « Thou shalt enter into the grave in abundance. (Ibid.) The grave is contemplation, etc., as far as what they may pour back for the profit of their neighbors. « As a heap of wheat is brought in. » Because the time of action is first, that of contemplation last. And it is needful that the perfect man first exercise his mind in virtues, and afterward lay it up in the barn of quiet. Or the grave 772Ais eternal rest, in which the more truly is rest had, the more perfectly the life of corruption is slain. Into this he enters in abundance who, after the works of this life are gathered in, being fully dead to this changeableness, is hidden in the secret of the true light, like wheat: because the just feel the strokes in another's time, but in their own, like grains, they enter into the barn.

VERS. 27.-- « We have searched it out. » In this he notes of himself that he had not spoken according to the surface concerning the grave, the beasts, the stones, the tabernacle, and the like, but that he had sought inward things in them. « What thou hast heard, ponder in thy mind. » Words of boasting; and though he taught well, it is unskillfulness to wish to teach a better man: whence the sayings of the friends, though right, are proved to have been not rightly said.

CAPUT VI.

772BVERS. 2.-- « Utinam appenderentur. » Tactus flagellis verbis lacessitur, non ut non erectus in defensionem contumax caderet: sed ut percussus tacens gratias egit, lacessitus recta respondit: « utinam appenderentur peccata. »

« Statera. » (Ibid.) In manu patris Christus habens lancem misericordiae et justitiae, peccatum per misericordiam laxans, leve esse; calamitatem moriendo gravis esse ponderis ostendit. Cui et hoc gratiae dat, ut ipsa cognoscibilis sit calamitas. Caecus enim homo in exsilio, quasi in patria, gaudebat: sed Deus extra apparens hominem intus revocat, ut viso quod amisit, grave doleat quod tolerat; dicit ergo:

« Utinam appenderentur. » Id est, malum nostrae, etc., usque ad pensans judicia Dei quibus affligitur, 772Cet hoc est:

VERS. 3.-- « Unde et verba mea dolore sunt plena, quia sagittae Domini. » Id est, percussio animadversionis Dei, quae non me, ut multos, inemundatum dimittit.

VERS. 4.-- « Ebibit spiritum. » (GREG.) Id est, elationem compescit, ut dicat: « Defecit spiritus meus (Psal. LXXVI). » Et non solum gemo quod patior, sed timeo quod minaris, et hoc est:

« Terrores Domini militant: » ne post hoc mors aeterna sequatur. Sed quia staturae adventum desiderat, quo ardore eumdem gentilitas, et Judaea sitiat, supponit, cum ait:

VERS. 5.-- « Nunquid rugiet onager, » id est, agrestis asinus, etc., usque ad sed carnaliter tenebatur, 772Dsubdit:

VERS. 6.-- « Aut poterit comedi insulsum. » Sal est virtus occultae intelligentiae, etc., usque ad convertendo esuriens recepit.

(Ibid.) Ille desiderat stateram, nos jam per fidem ejus vivimus; sed tamen pro purgatione sagittas ejus patimur, et terrore futuri timemus.

Sed quia filii sumus, non debemus timere ut Dominum, sed diligere ut Patrem, et ad eum anhelare, ubi cibo, id est, aeternitate ejus pascamur, pro quo modo rugimus, unde:

« Nunquid rugiet onager. » (Ibid.) Onager, est populus qui, etc., usque ad etiam ab hujusmodi verbis se separant, et hoc est:

773A« Aut poterit comedi insulsum. » Sed jam in alto positi, a stultis semoti, saepe dum sola propria ad ferendum dura procurant, pro amore veritatis non sunt accincti.

« Aut potest aliquis gustare. » (GREG.) Durum est petere quod cruciat, quod vitam fugat, sed saepe cum fit in alto, ad ferenda stultorum mala se inclinat, ut eos trahat. Aucto enim desiderio coelestium angustiatur, et prae dulcedine illorum amara saeculi non metuit. Unde, et post impossibilem mortis gustum, subdit:

VERS. 7.-- « Quae prius nolebat tangere, » id est, mala saeculi jam sunt dulces cibi prae amore coeli. Et cum haec dulcia sint, « Quis det ut veniat petitio mea? »

773BVERS. 9.-- « Qui coepit, ipse me conterat. » (GREG. in Job lib. VII.) Conterit adversarius, et defecit a te homo. Conterit Deus, et fractus a vitiis in virtute solidatur. Qui ergo per flagella Deo appropriare desiderat, dicit: « Qui coepit, ipse conterat, » quia aliquando incipit Deus contritionem vitiorum, et in ipso provectus initio homo extollitur, et sic hostis exclusus, gravior assumptis aliis spiritibus surripit, et virtutes conterit.

« Solvat manum, » id est, exerceat effectum; dum enim inflatus longa prosperitate amorem suum per flagella non exercet, quasi manum ad ferienda vitia ligatam tenet, unde peccanti populo dicit: Jam non irascar tibi, et zelus meus recessit a te.

« Succidat me. » Cum enim dolor vel tentatio non 773Cpercutit, elatio mentis succisa cadit, ut jam quaerat manum levantis.

VERS. 10.--Et haec mihi sit consolatio ut affligens. » (Ibid.) Quasi quibusdam hic parcit, ut aeternum feriat; sed me hic non parcens feriat, quod est mihi consolatio. Timent enim sancti hic sibi parci. Quod haec non tumenti, sed humili mente dixit, ostendit in his.

« Nec contradicam sermonibus. » Sermones Dei ad nos: non modo dicta, sed et facta, ut hic percussio Job; cui murmurando non contradicit, quia et quem feriendo tolerat, sanctum vocat.

VERS. 11.--Quae est enim fortitudo mea? » (Ibid.) Alia est fortitudo bonorum, etc., usque ad et pro ea quam non amat, pati recusat, dicit ergo:

773D« Quae est enim fortitudo, aut quis finis? » Id est, mala, etc., usque ad unde infirmus habet vires, unde subjungit:

VERS. 13.-- « Ecce non est auxilium. » Ad majoris quoque meritum fortitudinis, quomodo a proximis destituitur, addit:

« Necessarii quoque. » Si exterius despectus, intus solio judicii praesidet; nam ad sententias erumpit: « Qui tollit, etc. »

VERS. 14.-- « Qui tollit ab amico suo. » (Ibid.) Amicus est quilibet proximus, etc., usque ad qui intus dudum aeterna negligendo perierunt: de quibus recte dicitur:

VERS. 17.-- « Et ut incaluerint, » id est, cum per 774Acognitionem poenae fervere coeperint, a delectatione carnis, quam desiderio tenebant, separabuntur. Mali aeterna non intelligunt, nisi cum jam pro terrenis puniuntur: tunc mens aestuat, et igne serae poenitentiae se inflammat; tunc ejus duritia per supplicium liquatur, unde propheta: « Tantummodo vexatio sola intellectum dabit auditui » (Isai. XXVIII), hoc tunc passuri, interim his implicantur.

VERS. 18.-- « Involutae sunt. » Omne quod involvitur in se replicatur: sic mali, volentes aliquando bona quasi extra se tensi, ad consueta mala tentati replicantur. Vel involutae sunt, qui etsi aliquando victa una nequitia pedem levant, regnans altera et ad eam quam vicerant implicat, ut qui jam spreta avaritia adhuc luxuriosus est, pro pretio luxuriae ad 774Bavaritiam redit. Sic ope vicaria fugitivum suum vitia retinent, et quasi laqueis egressus ligant, unde saepe lapsi ad suas voluptates redeunt, nec curant ea quae permanent, unde subdit:

« Ambulabunt in vacuum et peribunt. » Quia nihil secum de fructu sui laboris portant. Sed justus in conspectu Domini non apparet vacuus: portat enim manipulos justitiae. Ab illorum autem imitatione per finem terret, « Considerate semitas, etc. »

VERS. 19.-- « Considerate semitas. » (Ibid.) Thema interpretatur auster, Saba rete: Auster qui tepore membra dissolvit, est hic fluxa vivendi remissio; rete est actionis obligatio, et fluxis actibus homo implicatur, dum non perfecte mundum deserit, dum non districto studio, sed dissolutis gressibus vadit, 774Cut qui pro amore carnali parentum ad ea quae Dei sunt praepeditur. « Considerate, » dicit, quia perversa agendo diligimus, sed haec visa in aliis dijudicamus, unde ad nos reversi de hujusmodi erubescimus.

« Exspectate paulisper. » Quia parum est quidquid finitur; sed qui hoc longum putat, de aeternis non curat. Dicta brevitate vitae contra iniquos insurgit voce justorum

VERS. 20.-- « Confusi sunt. » Quia scilicet mali bonos per aspera a spe conantur retrahere: quod si peragunt, exsultant; si non, incassum crudeles fuisse erubescunt; unde et quid in retributione fiat, subdit:

« Venerunt quoque. » In judicio mali ad justos perveniunt, quia eorum gloriam videbunt, ut gravius 774Ddoleant videntes quod perdiderunt. Tunc pudor eos operit, cum testis conscientia urit. Eadem spiritualiter. Amici Job confusi sunt, quia dum eum ad desperationem inflectere nequeunt, confunduntur, venerunt dum jam mentis constantiam intus agnoscunt et erubescunt. Quos ostendit de numero eorum qui Deum non timent, nisi suis vel aliorum adversis territi.

VERS. 21.-- « Nunc venistis, et modo videntes plagam meam. » (Ibid.) Quasi, ego Deum et ante adversa timui, vos qui ex amore Deum non timetis, ex solo verbere formidatis.

VERS. 22.-- « Nunquid dixi. » Ecclesia substantia haereticorum (quorum figura sunt amici Job) non 775Aeget, id est, carnali sapientia; et recta, quae contra diabolum de conversione aliquando loquuntur, audire ab eis renuit, quia per haec ad perfidiam trahunt. « Manus hostis » est fortitudo Satanae. « Robusti » sunt maligni spiritus.

« Nunquid dixi: Afferte. » Contra divites mundi, etc., usque ad et quod in seipsis despiciunt, et in aliis.

VERS. 24.-- « Docete me. » Hoc vel prioribus subjungitur, vel per se dicitur per increpationem.

VERS. 25.-- « Quare detraxistis. » (GREG.) Mundus esse debet, etc., usque ad reprehensibilia profertis ad increpandum?

(Ibid.) Duo genera locutionis noxia sunt, quod et perversa laudat et quod etiam recta vituperat; ibi 775Bfavor, hic ira: de hoc amicos Job arguit; ad quod vitium ex otiosis dictis pervenitur, unde subdit:

VERS. 26.-- « Et in ventum. » In ventum verba proferre, etc., usque ad sit ergo ostium linguae quod modo claudatur, modo aperiatur.

VERS. 27.-- « Super pupillum. » (GREG. in Job lib. VIII.) Ecce quantae infirmitatis se esse perpendat, etc., usque ad si tolerat quos non amat, vel si non tolerat quos amat.

VERS. 28.-- « Videte an mentiar. » (Ibid.) Quia humilis, quae dicit, non ex auctoritate praecipit, sed ex ratione persuadet; sed quia occasione rationis, contrarii ad contentionem effrenantur, subdit.

VERS. 29.-- « Respondete, obsecro absque contentione. » Quia haeretici inquisitionibus non intendunt 775Cveritatem assequi, sed victores videri.
