« Et loquentes, etc. » Quia qui loquitur, judicio auditoris subditur. Inter cor et linguam ejus aequus arbiter sit, ut nihil praecipitanter de corde per linguam prodeat, sed prius examinet quam dicat. Et quia qui prius de suis, recte judicant de dictis alienis, addit:

VERS. 30.-- « Et non invenietis in lingua mea. » Quia non vobis in me stultitia sonat, si a vestra conscientia non procedat; sic Ecclesia prius falsa hostium destruit, post vera aperit, quibus non est locus dum falsa pro veris habentur; per « linguam » aperta locutio, per « fauces » occulta tractatio, quia quod Ecclesia foris praedicat, vivendo custodit.

CHAPTER VI.

772BVERS. 2.-- « Would that they were weighed. » Touched by the scourges, he is provoked by words, not that, not raised up into his own defence, he might fall defiant: but that, struck, he kept silence and gave thanks, and provoked, answered rightly: « would that sins were weighed. »

« A balance. » (Ibid.) Christ, holding in the Father's hand the scale of mercy and of justice, loosing sin through mercy, showed it to be light; and by dying showed calamity to be of heavy weight. To him he grants this also of grace, that the calamity itself should be knowable. For man, being blind, rejoiced in exile as though in his homeland: but God, appearing without, calls man back within, so that, having seen what he lost, he may grieve heavily for what he endures; he says therefore:

« Would that they were weighed. » That is, the evil of our, etc., as far as weighing the judgments of God by which he is afflicted, 772Cand this is:

VERS. 3.-- « Whence also my words are full of sorrow, because the arrows of the Lord. » That is, the stroke of God's chastisement, which does not dismiss me uncleansed, as it does many.

VERS. 4.-- « It hath drunk up the spirit. » (GREG.) That is, it curbs pride, so that he may say: « My spirit hath failed (Psal. LXXVI). » And I not only groan at what I suffer, but I fear what thou threatenest, and this is:

« The terrors of the Lord war: » lest after this eternal death should follow. But because he longs for the coming of the stature, with what ardour the Gentile world and Judaea thirst for the same he sets down, when he says:

VERS. 5.-- « Will the onager bray, » that is, the wild ass, etc., as far as but he was held carnally, 772Dhe adds:

VERS. 6.-- « Or can that which is unsavoury be eaten. » Salt is the power of hidden understanding, etc., as far as hungering, he received it by being converted.

(Ibid.) He longs for the balance; we now live by his faith; but yet for our cleansing we suffer his arrows, and with dread of what is to come we fear.

But because we are sons, we ought not to fear him as a Master, but to love him as a Father, and to pant after him, where we may be fed with food, that is, with his eternity, for which we now bray, whence:

« Will the onager bray. » (Ibid.) The onager is the people who, etc., as far as they separate themselves even from words of this kind, and this is:

773A« Or can that which is unsavoury be eaten. » But those already set on high, removed from the foolish, often, while they see to their own concerns alone, hard for the bearing, are not girded for the love of truth.

« Or can anyone taste. » (GREG.) It is hard to ask for what torments, what puts life to flight; but often, when he is set on high, he bends himself to bearing the evils of the foolish, that he may draw them. For with the desire of heavenly things increased he is straitened, and before their sweetness he does not fear the bitter things of the world. Whence, after the impossible taste of death, he adds:

VERS. 7.-- « Which before it would not touch, » that is, the evils of the world are now sweet food before the love of heaven. And since these are sweet, « Who will grant that my petition may come? »

773BVERS. 9.-- « May he who has begun, himself crush me. » (GREG. in Job lib. VII.) The adversary crushes, and man falls away from thee. God crushes, and one broken off from his vices is made solid in virtue. He therefore who through scourges desires to draw near to God says: « May he who has begun, himself crush, » because sometimes God begins the crushing of vices, and in that very beginning of advance the man is lifted up, and thus the enemy, shut out, creeps in the heavier with other spirits taken to himself, and crushes the virtues.

« Let him loose his hand, » that is, let him put forth the effect; for while, puffed up with long prosperity, he does not exercise his love through scourges, he holds his hand as it were bound for striking the vices; whence he says to a sinning people: I will no longer be angry with thee, and my zeal is departed from thee.

« Let him cut me off. » For when sorrow or temptation does not 773Cstrike, the elation of the mind falls cut down, so that it now seeks the hand of him who lifts up.

VERS. 10.--And let this be my consolation, that afflicting. » (Ibid.) As it were: here he spares certain men, that he may strike them eternally; but let him strike me here, not sparing, which is a consolation to me. For the saints fear to be spared here. That he said this not with a swelling but with a humble mind, he shows in these words.

« Nor will I contradict his words. » God's words to us are not only things said, but also things done, as here the striking of Job; which he does not contradict by murmuring, because him also whom he bears with by striking, he calls holy.

VERS. 11.--For what is my strength? » (Ibid.) The strength of the good is one thing, etc., as far as and for that which he does not love he refuses to suffer; he says therefore:

773D« For what is my strength, or what my end? » That is, evils, etc., as far as whence the weak man has strength; whence he subjoins:

VERS. 13.-- « Behold there is no help. » To the merit also of a greater fortitude he adds how he is forsaken by those nearest him:

« My kinsfolk also. » Though despised without, within he presides on the throne of judgment; for he breaks out into sentences: « He that taketh away, etc. »

VERS. 14.-- « He that taketh away from his friend. » (Ibid.) A friend is any neighbour, etc., as far as who within have long since perished by neglecting eternal things: of whom it is rightly said:

VERS. 17.-- « And when they shall have grown warm, » that is, when through 774Athe knowledge of punishment they shall have begun to burn, they will be separated from the delight of the flesh which they held by desire. The wicked do not understand eternal things, save when they are already punished for earthly ones: then the mind boils, and inflames itself with the fire of late repentance; then its hardness is melted by torment, whence the prophet: « Vexation alone shall give understanding to the hearing » (Isai. XXVIII); and being about to suffer this then, meanwhile they are entangled in these things.

VERS. 18.-- « They are wound about. » Everything that is wound about is folded back upon itself: so the wicked, sometimes willing good things as though stretched outside themselves, are folded back, when tempted, to their accustomed evils. Or they are wound about who, even if sometimes, one wickedness being conquered, they lift their foot, another wickedness reigning entangles them even toward that which they had conquered: as he who, avarice being now spurned, is still lustful, and for the price of his lust returns to 774Bavarice. Thus by mutual help the vices retain their fugitive, and bind his goings out as with snares; whence, often fallen, they return to their pleasures, and care not for the things that abide; whence he adds:

« They shall walk in vain and shall perish. » Because they carry nothing with them of the fruit of their labour. But the just man does not appear empty in the sight of the Lord: for he carries the sheaves of justice. And from imitating them he terrifies by their end: « Consider the paths, etc. »

VERS. 19.-- « Consider the paths. » (Ibid.) Thema is interpreted the south wind, Saba a net: the south wind, which loosens the limbs by its warmth, is here the loose slackening of living; the net is the binding of action, and by loose deeds a man is entangled, while he does not perfectly forsake the world, while he goes not with strict zeal but with dissolute steps, 774Cas one who for carnal love of parents is hindered from the things that are God's. « Consider, » he says, because in doing perverse things we love them, but seeing these in others we judge them; whence, returned to ourselves, we blush for such things.

« Wait a little while. » Because whatever is ended is little; but he who thinks this long has no care for eternal things. The brevity of life being told, he rises up against the wicked with the voice of the just

VERS. 20.-- « They are confounded. » Because, namely, the wicked try through hardships to draw the good back from hope: and if they accomplish it, they exult; if not, they blush to have been cruel in vain; whence he adds also what comes to pass in the retribution:

« They came also. » In the judgment the wicked come through to the just, because they shall see their glory, that they may grieve the more heavily 774Dseeing what they have lost. Then shame covers them, when conscience as witness burns them. The same spiritually. Job's friends are confounded, because while they cannot bend him to despair, they are confounded; they came, while they now inwardly acknowledge the constancy of his mind and blush. These he shows to be of the number of those who do not fear God, unless terrified by their own adversities or those of others.

VERS. 21.-- « Now you are come, and now seeing my wound. » (Ibid.) As if to say: I feared God even before adversities; you who do not fear God out of love, dread him from the lash alone.

VERS. 22.-- « Have I said. » The Church has no need 775Aof the substance of heretics (whose figure Job's friends are), that is, of carnal wisdom; and it refuses to hear from them even the right things which they sometimes speak against the devil concerning conversion, because through these they draw men to perfidy. « The hand of the enemy » is the strength of Satan. « The mighty » are the malignant spirits.

« Have I said: Bring. » Against the rich of the world, etc., as far as and what they despise in themselves, also in others.

VERS. 24.-- « Teach me. » This is either subjoined to what precedes, or is said by itself by way of rebuke.

VERS. 25.-- « Why have you detracted. » (GREG.) He ought to be clean, etc., as far as do you bring forward reprehensible things for rebuking?

(Ibid.) Two kinds of speech are harmful: that which praises perverse things, and that which blames even right ones; there 775Bis favour, here anger: for this he reproves Job's friends; and one arrives at that vice from idle words, whence he adds:

VERS. 26.-- « And into the wind. » To utter words into the wind, etc., as far as let there be therefore a door of the tongue, which is now shut, now opened.

VERS. 27.-- « Upon the orphan. » (GREG. in Job lib. VIII.) See of how great weakness he should weigh himself to be, etc., as far as whether he bears with those whom he does not love, or does not bear with those whom he loves.

VERS. 28.-- « See whether I lie. » (Ibid.) Because, being humble, the things he says he does not enjoin from authority, but persuades from reason; but because on occasion of reason his opponents are unbridled into contention, he adds.

VERS. 29.-- « Answer, I beseech you, without contention. » Because heretics in their inquiries do not aim 775Cto attain the truth, but to seem victors.

« And speaking, etc. » Because he who speaks is subjected to the judgment of the hearer. Between his heart and his tongue let there be a fair arbiter, that nothing may come forth headlong from the heart through the tongue, but that he examine before he speaks. And because those who first judge of their own, judge rightly of the sayings of others, he adds:

VERS. 30.-- « And you shall not find in my tongue. » Because folly does not sound to you in me, if it does not proceed from your own conscience; so the Church first destroys the false things of her enemies, afterwards opens the true, for which there is no place while false things are held for true; by « the tongue » is open speech, by « the throat » hidden handling, because what the Church preaches without, she keeps by living.

CAPUT VII.

775DVERS. 1.-- « Et sicut dies mercenarii. » (GREG.) Qui dies cito transire optat, ut ad laboris praemium veniat, sic omnis justus; et sicut ille in alienis laboribus sudat, sed proprium praemium sibi parat, sic justus in mundanis malis sudet, ut coelestia bona capiat. Cavet ne vacuus ab opere dies eat, et praemium minuatur. Patitur dura, ut magis coronetur, licet merces jam sit in corde; sed tamen gravius aestus sentitur in corpore, ut post sit refrigerium in quiete, et hoc est:

VERS. 2.-- « Sicut servus. » Bene omnis electus servus dicitur, quamdiu sub jugo corruptionis hujus est, antequam veniat in libertatem gloriae.

« Et sicut mercenarius. » Qui de gravitate operis 776Alacessit, nisi spe finis relevaretur, sed illo viso nihil putat quod sentit.

VERS. 3.-- « Menses vacuos. » A rebus mundi, quia non eas quaerunt per actus suos boni, et noctes adversitatum tolerant laboriosas, quando jam sunt in cruciatu corporis. Vel menses vacui, quia modo est labor, sed nondum praemium.

« Noctes laboriosas. » Enumerat, etc., usque ad eo magis gemit diu nescisse quod quaerit, et quod inaniter laboravit.

VERS. 4.-- « Si dormiero. » (Ibid.) Secundum historiam animus dolentis exprimitur, qui, cogente moestitia, per diversa desideria vagatur; in nocte diem, in die vesperam desiderat, quia dolor non sinit placere quod adest; et jam hoc expertus grave, 776Bconsolante desiderio aliud exspectat; sed nec sic dolor finitur, unde:

« Et replebor doloribus. » Causa autem doloris haec est:

VERS. 5.-- « Induta est. » (Ibid.) Eadem subtilius in Ecclesia, etc., usque ad non eis est hic integra salus, unde subdit:

« Et replebor doloribus usque ad tenebras. » Quia securitatem non invenit, donec penitus tempus tentationis relinquit. Doloris autem causam subdit:

« Induta est caro, » id est, carnalis vita quam ago, vel mala operatione polluitur, vel inde restricta, ex memoria imaginibus peccatorum fuscatur, quae quasi pulvis ante oculos surgunt. Ecclesia vero 776Cin quibusdam putredinem luxuriae carnis sentit, in quibus terrenis actibus deditis pulvere conspergitur: de utrisque subdit:

« Cutis mea. » Cutis sunt in Ecclesia, qui solis exterioribus curis vacant: quorum corda desperatio siccat, et ideo contrahuntur, non extenti per longanimitatem ad aeternam vitam: praesentibus haerent: quod ideo fit, quia non attendunt quam fugitiva haec vita sit. Sed Ecclesia econtra:

VERS. 6.-- « Dies mei velocius. » Sicut tela filis, sic vita diebus singulis proficit: sed quo magis crescit, ad incisionem tendit. Sed et telae festinantiam velocitas vitae transit, quae semper deficit. Et ideo electi cor non in ea figunt, unde ait:

« Et consumpti sunt, » id est, nullam mihi fiduciam 776Dpono in his: quod faciunt reprobi, ego non.

VERS. 7.-- « Memento. » (Ibid.) Id est, benignus velociter transeuntem respice, quia humanae brevitatis consideratio, Deo est grata oblatio.

« Quia ventus est. » Vita aeterna et solida: hic vita nec solida nec manens judicatur.

(Ibid.) Et quia postquam transit vita, non est locus merendi veniam, subdit:

« Et non revertitur. » Id est, post mortem, mens suam retributionem videns, non revocatur ad exhibenda bona opera: unde dives de se desperans, quod est aggravatio poenae, pro fratribus rogat. Ecce vita transit, quod sequitur permanet. Post etiam,

777AVERS. 8.-- « Nec aspiciet me visus hominis. » Id est misericordia Redemptoris, quae peccantium duritiam emollit, ut cum Petrum respexit (Luc. XXII); sed post mortem non liberat, quem ante gratia non reformat. Non visus hominis, sed,

« Oculi tui in. » (GERG.) Id est, districtum judicem ad feriendum videbis, et qui hic exspectatus ad poenitentiam substiti, jam non subsistat. Hoc justo convenit, qui timet omne quod agit, et remota pietate se periturum praescit, et non subsistit ante oculos, quia poenae vita non sufficit, si justitia eum premit. Nec enim astra, id est qui sanctitate lucent, coram eo munda sunt. Culpa autem et poena humani generis breviter subditur.

VERS. 9.-- « Sicut consumitur nubes. » (Ibid.) Nubes 777Bad alta levatur, etc., usque ad vade in domum tuam.

VERS. 10.-- « Neque cognoscet eum amplius locus. » (Ibid.) Locus hominis Deus est, etc., usque ad adhuc de corruptione carnis contradictionem sentit.

VERS. 12.-- « Nunquid mare sum. » (Ibid.) Carcer, est caro quae impedit, etc., usque ad nunc subdit consilium contra ea.

VERS. 15.-- « Quamobrem elegit. » (Ibid.) Anima, est intentio mentis, etc., usque ad « si hoc mihi facturus es interfice me. »

VERS. 16.-- « Parce mihi, Domine, » ut aeterna des, quia contemptu illorum haec melius sperantur. Dum parci sibi petit, probat quod non a Deo desperavit; 777Cquod est, suspendium elegit, hoc est, desperavit, de eodem intulit, parce: quod est, mortem ossa: hoc est, non ultra vivant hoc tertio.

« Nihil enim sunt dies mei. » Qui aeterna considerat, ad se rediens bene hos dies nihil vocat, et districte judicari formidat.

VERS. 17.-- « Quid est homo. » (Ibid.) Magnificat Deus hominem multo spirituali munere, et erga eum ponit cor, quia pro datis districtius judicat. Sed quid homo? quasi: etsi magnificatus ultra se donis tuis, tamen quia caro est, si sine pietate judicetur, ferre non potest, unde adhuc addit:

VERS. 18.-- « Visitas eum diluculo. » Diluculum est cum de nocte vitiorum et ignorantiae ad lucem venit homo, in quo et mox probatur: ne enim bona 777Da se habere glorietur, tentatione concuti permittitur, ut altitudo Dei et sui infirmitas videatur, sicut Salomon post sapientiam tentatur a meretricibus; haec tota hac mortalitate homo patitur unde: « Usquequo non parcis mihi. »

VERS. 19.-- « Salivam. » (Ibid.) Saliva in os a capite labitur, etc., usque ad qui hoc vult mereri, fatetur malum quod fecit: « Peccavi, etc. »

VERS. 20.-- « Peccavi, quid faciam tibi. » (Ibid.) Quasi: quid in recompensationem tibi offeram non invenio, quia omnis virtus hominis ad culpam abluendam infirma est. Vel, « quid faciam tibi, » quasi: omnia bona non tibi, sed nobis prosunt.

« O custos hominum. » Quia nos sic destituit, ut 778Anos custodire nequeamus, et si quid conamur, peccatis gravamur, unde:

« Quare posuisti me contrarium. » Cum homo serpenti credidit, etc., usque ad sed misericordiam quaerit, unde addit:

VERS. 21.-- « Cur non tollis. » Ecce desiderium Mediatoris, qui tollit peccata mundi. Vel, tunc peccatum plene tolletur, cum in corruptionem homo mutabitur. Desiderat ergo Redemptorem vel resurrectionem, unde et poenam quam ex origine meruit, et judicium quod ex propria actione timet, adjungit. Ecce nunc peccanti homini dictum est: Cinis es, et in cinerem reverteris. »

« Quare posuisti me contrarium. » (Ibid.) Quia inferior, factus contrarius modo superiori, id est 778BDeo: ideo nostrum inferius, id est sensualitas, factum est contrarium superiori, id est spiritui, ut semper pugnent.

« Mane, » id est, in adventu judicis, cui omnia aperta. Quaerere Dei, est districte judicare, ante quem nec justus subsistit; sed hoc est solatium liberationis, quod se sufficere non posse dicit humilis; plangit ergo mortem quae nunc est, timet judicium quod imminet.

CHAPTER VII.

775DVERS. 1.-- « And like the days of a hireling. » (GREG.) He who wishes the day to pass quickly, that he may come to the reward of his labour, so is every just man; and as that man sweats in another's labours but prepares his own reward for himself, so let the just man sweat amid worldly evils, that he may lay hold of heavenly goods. He takes care lest the day go empty of work, and the reward be lessened. He suffers hard things, that he may be the more crowned, although the wage is already in his heart; but yet the heat is felt more heavily in the body, that afterwards there may be refreshment in rest, and this is:

VERS. 2.-- « As a servant. » Every elect man is rightly called a servant, so long as he is under the yoke of this corruption, before he comes into the liberty of glory.

« And like a hireling. » Who chafes at the heaviness of the work, 776Aunless he were relieved by hope of the end; but when that is seen, he counts as nothing what he feels.

VERS. 3.-- « Empty months. » Empty of the things of the world, because the good do not seek them through their acts; and they endure toilsome nights of adversities, when they are already in the torment of the body. Or the months are empty, because now there is labour, but not yet the reward.

« Toilsome nights. » He numbers, etc., as far as the more he groans that he was long ignorant of what he seeks, and that he laboured in vain.

VERS. 4.-- « If I sleep. » (Ibid.) According to the history, the mind of one grieving is expressed, who, sadness compelling him, wanders through diverse desires; in the night he desires the day, in the day the evening, because grief does not let what is present please him; and having now found this heavy, 776Bwith desire consoling him he awaits another thing; but not even so is the grief ended, whence:

« And I shall be filled with sorrows. » And the cause of the sorrow is this:

VERS. 5.-- « It is clothed. » (Ibid.) The same things more subtly in the Church, etc., as far as they have not here entire health, whence he adds:

« And I shall be filled with sorrows until the darkness. » Because he does not find security until he wholly leaves the time of temptation. And he adds the cause of the sorrow:

« The flesh is clothed, » that is, the carnal life which I lead is either defiled by evil working, or, held back from that, is darkened out of memory by the images of sins, which rise like dust before the eyes. But the Church 776Cfeels in some the rottenness of the flesh's lust, and in those who are given to earthly acts is sprinkled with dust: of both he adds:

« My skin. » The skin in the Church are they who are free only for outward cares: whose hearts despair dries up, and therefore they are drawn together, not stretched out through long-suffering toward eternal life: they cling to things present: which comes about because they do not attend how fleeting this life is. But the Church on the contrary:

VERS. 6.-- « My days more swiftly. » As a web grows by threads, so life advances by single days: but the more it grows, the more it tends toward the cutting. But the swiftness of life outstrips even the haste of the web, and always fails. And therefore the elect do not fix their heart in it, whence he says:

« And they are consumed, » that is, I place no 776Dconfidence for myself in these things: what the reprobate do, I do not.

VERS. 7.-- « Remember. » (Ibid.) That is, look kindly upon him who passes swiftly, because the consideration of human brevity is an offering pleasing to God.

« Because it is wind. » Eternal life is also solid: here life is judged neither solid nor abiding.

(Ibid.) And because after life passes there is no place for meriting pardon, he adds:

« And it returneth not. » That is, after death the mind, seeing its own retribution, is not called back to the performing of good works: whence the rich man, despairing of himself, which is an aggravation of the punishment, prays for his brethren. Behold, life passes; what follows abides. Afterwards also,

777AVERS. 8.-- « Nor shall the sight of man behold me. » That is, the mercy of the Redeemer, which softens the hardness of sinners, as when he looked upon Peter (Luc. XXII); but after death it does not free him whom grace does not reform before. Not the sight of man, but,

« Thine eyes upon. » (GERG.) That is, thou shalt see a strict judge to strike, and he who here, awaited unto repentance, stood firm substiti, let him now not stand firm. This befits the just man, who fears everything that he does, and knows beforehand that, mercy removed, he will perish, and does not stand before those eyes, because his life is not sufficient for the punishment, if justice presses him. For neither are the stars, that is, they who shine with holiness, clean before him. And the fault and the punishment of the human race is briefly added.

VERS. 9.-- « As a cloud is consumed. » (Ibid.) A cloud 777Bis lifted on high, etc., as far as go into thy house.

VERS. 10.-- « Neither shall his place know him any more. » (Ibid.) Man's place is God, etc., as far as he still feels contradiction from the corruption of the flesh.

VERS. 12.-- « Am I a sea. » (Ibid.) The prison is the flesh which hinders, etc., as far as now he adds counsel against them.

VERS. 15.-- « Wherefore it hath chosen. » (Ibid.) The soul is the intention of the mind, etc., as far as « if thou art about to do this to me, kill me. »

VERS. 16.-- « Spare me, O Lord, » that thou mayest give eternal things, because by contempt of those these are the better hoped for. While he asks to be spared, he proves that he did not despair of God; 777Cwhich is, he chose hanging, that is, he despaired; of the same he brought in, spare: which is, death the bones: that is, let them not live further, this the third time.

« For my days are nothing. » He who considers eternal things, returning to himself, rightly calls these days nothing, and dreads to be strictly judged.

VERS. 17.-- « What is man. » (Ibid.) God magnifies man with much spiritual gift, and sets his heart toward him, because for what is given he judges the more strictly. But what is man? as if to say: even though magnified beyond himself by thy gifts, yet because he is flesh, if he be judged without mercy he cannot bear it; whence he further adds:

VERS. 18.-- « Thou visitest him at daybreak. » Daybreak is when man comes out of the night of vices and of ignorance into the light, in which he is also straightway tested: for lest he boast that he has good things 777Dfrom himself, he is allowed to be shaken by temptation, that the height of God and his own weakness may be seen, as Solomon after his wisdom is tempted by harlots; all this man suffers in this mortality, whence: « How long dost thou not spare me. »

VERS. 19.-- « Spittle. » (Ibid.) Spittle slips into the mouth from the head, etc., as far as he who wishes to merit this confesses the evil which he has done: « I have sinned, etc. »

VERS. 20.-- « I have sinned, what shall I do to thee. » (Ibid.) As if to say: I find not what I may offer thee in recompense, because every strength of man is too weak to wash away guilt. Or, « what shall I do to thee, » as if to say: all good things profit not thee, but us.

« O keeper of men. » Because he so forsook us that we 778Acannot keep ourselves, and if we attempt anything, we are weighed down by sins, whence:

« Why hast thou set me contrary. » When man believed the serpent, etc., as far as but he seeks mercy, whence he adds:

VERS. 21.-- « Why dost thou not take away. » Behold the desire of the Mediator, who taketh away the sins of the world. Or, then will sin be fully taken away, when man shall be changed into corruption [in corruptionem; read incorruptionem, "incorruption"]. He desires therefore the Redeemer, or the resurrection; whence he adds both the punishment which he merited from his origin, and the judgment which he fears from his own action. Behold, it was now said to sinning man: Ashes thou art, and into ashes thou shalt return. » [Vulg. pulvis es et in pulverem reverteris, "dust thou art, and unto dust thou shalt return"]

« Why hast thou set me contrary. » (Ibid.) Because the lower, having been made in a manner contrary to the higher, that is 778Bto God: therefore our lower part, that is sensuality, has been made contrary to the higher, that is to the spirit, so that they always fight.

« In the morning, » that is, at the coming of the judge, to whom all things are open. God's seeking is to judge strictly, before whom not even the just man stands; but this is the solace of deliverance, that the humble man says he cannot suffice for himself; he laments therefore the death which now is, he fears the judgment which is at hand.

CAPUT VIII.

VERS. 1.-- « Respondens autem. » (GREG.) Sciendum est imprimis, quia sicut Eliphaz sanctissimum Job de superbia principaliter notavit, licet et de hypocrisi breviter tetigerit, ita et iste de hypocrisi principaliter notare intendit; sed priusquam hoc 778Cfaciat, quiddam praemittit unde perpenditur quam onerosa ei fuerint verba ipsius Job, verba scilicet aedificationis: quasi ergo ulterius bene loquentem pati non possit, indignative in haec verba prorupit, dicens:

VERS. 2.-- « Usquequo loqueris talia. » Quasi dicat: quare loqueris inutilia? et spiritus multiplex . . . . .

« Usque loqueris talia. » (Ibid.) Quia gravia sunt iniquo verba aedificationis quasi ferre non possit; et quia non vult corrigi, bene dicta criminatur.

« Et spiritus multiplex. » (Ibid.) Dum multiplicitatem sermoni ejus tribuit, inopiam intelligentiae ipsius reprehendit. Et nota esse quatuor genera loquendi. 778DAlii enim ampli sunt ore et sensu, qui laudandi sunt; alii vero utroque stricti, qui miserandi; alii sensu ampli, sed non possunt eloqui, qui sunt adjuvandi; alii sensu inopes, sed lingua torrentes, qui despiciendi: quod iste in crimen Job torquet.

VERS. 3.-- « Nunquid Deus. » (GREG.) Haec Job nec negaverat nec ignorabat, etc., usque ad quasi juste perierint, alios feriunt, unde subdit:

VERS. 4, 5.-- « Etiamsi filii tui peccaverunt, etc. Si diluculo: » quasi ipsi in luce, Ecclesia sit in nocte: quam vocant, ut per cognitionem Dei surgat, et per precem poenitentiae praeterita diluat, « si mundus » in cogitatione, « rectus » in opere.

779AVERS. 6.-- « Evigilabit. » Qui ab adjutorio tui modo errantis dormit.

« Et pacatum reddet. » (GREG.) Id est praesentis vitae tranquillitatem restituet, quam solam aestimant esse Dei remunerationem.

« Habitaculum justi. » (Ibid.) Consilium mentis dicunt quod pacatur, quia si eos ad sua trahunt, a contentione conticescunt. Opes quoque intelligentiae promittunt dicendo.

VERS. 7.-- « In tantum. » Sed quia verbis eorum non facile creditur, sententias patrum in argumentum sui erroris inflectunt, unde additur:

« Ut priora. » Quia et amissa recuperari, et adhuc majora praesentis vitae praemia promittunt.

VERS. 8.-- « Interroga enim genera. » Non videri, 779Bsed investigari monent, quia hoc nolunt conspici, quod liquido patet cunctis.

VERS. 9.-- « Hesterni: » aliquando moraliter docent ex praeteritis praesentia conjici, scilicet ex transitu eorum quod haec nulla sint, unde subdit, « hesterni. » Quia, si pristina recolitur generatio, haec vita cito transire ut umbra cognoscitur.

VERS. 10.-- « Ipsi docebunt. » Laudant patres nobiscum, unde magis nos impugnet.

« De corde. » (Ibid.) Quasi tu habes simplicitatem spiritus in ore, non in corde: illi contra. Saepe mali, se non attendentes, bonos lacerant, et vel bona, quae non videndo, sed audiendo didicerunt, proferunt; vel mala, quae ipsi agunt, aliis ingerunt, et ita factis se excusant, verbis judicant. Sic Baldath 779Csimulator justitiae contra hypocritas mira dicit, et de his docere justum praesumit, quae convenirent pravis. Vecors qui, hortis sitientibus, in flumine aquam fundit.

VERS. 11.-- « Nunquid vivere potest. » (Ibid.) Scirpo vel carecto hypocritam comparat, etc., usque ad sed alienis criminibus ostendat.

« Ante omnes herbas. » (Ibid.) Et justi herbae, secundum carnem, quia omnis caro fenum (Isa. XLVIII); qui arescunt, quia eorum opera cum carnis vita deficiunt. Sed hypocritarum ante: quia mox bona deserunt, ut ea consecuti sunt, propter quae simulaverunt. Hi sunt « fenum tectorum, quod priusquam evellatur, exaruit. »

VERS. 13.-- « Sic viae omnium. » Ecce cui comparat 779Dscirpum vel carectum: spes hypocritae est gloria, et hujusmodi saecularia, quae pereunt; aeterna permanent.

VERS. 14.-- « Non ei placebit vecordia. » (Ibid.) Vecordia est magnam rem vili pretio vendere, id est, bona laboriosa agere pro favore: hac modo delectatur, sed in poena displicebit, et tunc sciet nihil fuisse omnia quae transierunt, unde:

« Et sicut tela aranearum fiducia ejus. » Tela aranearum studiose texitur, flatu venti dissipatur: sic, quidquid hypocrita exsudat, favoris aura tollit.

VERS. 15.-- « Innitetur super domum suam. » Domus mentis, est quaelibet res quam per dilectionem habitat animus: domus ergo hypocritae, est 780Afavor. Vel, domus est fiducia sanctitatis, quae coram hominibus, sed non coram Deo stabit.

VERS. 16.-- « Humectus videtur. » Scirpus, hypocrita, in nocte humidus videtur, id est, gratia sanctitatis infusus apparet hominibus, sed coram sole in die judicii arescet.

« Et in ortu suo germen. » Quaelibet herba prius a terra producitur, etc., usque ad hypocrita, etiam cum recte agit, a multis vult videri.

VERS. 17.-- « Super acervum petrarum. » (Ibid.) Radices sunt cogitationes; lapides, quia non additur vivi, homines boni et mali sunt; hypocrita ergo qui inter lapides moratur, cogitationes in quaerenda admiratione hominum multiplicat: per omnia enim quae agit occulta cogitatione laudes quaerit, palam 780Bhabens quod hostes praedentur. Ut Ezechias, qui post multa beneficia sibi facta, nuntiis regis Babylonis omnia sua bona ostendit: unde mox audit: « Tollentur omnes in Babylonem (II Reg. XX); » ita et dum laus quaeritur, virtus et pulchritudo in manus inimici traditur, unde Baldad dicit:

« Et inter lapides commorabitur. » Quia ibi stat, ubi suae mentis intentionem solidat: qui etsi sit plenus bonis, si absorbuerit eum Deus, id est, per mortem tulerit, de loco, id est favoribus hujus vitae; dicet: non novi te, ut fatuis virginibus dicitur (Matth. XXV): et merito, quia non satis est ei nisi et alios sibi similes fecerit, et hoc est quod dicit: haec est enim laetitia viae ejus.

« Haec est enim laetitia, etc. (Ibid.) Ut et pro illorum 780Creatu puniatur, sed:

VERS. 20.-- « Deus non projiciet simplicem. » Cujus simplicitas modo a calliditate duplicium vocetur fatuitas.

« Nec porriget manum. » (Ibid.) Ut ab imo quo quaerunt gloriam elevet. Maligni sunt hypocritae qui nec bona bene agunt. Vel quia proximo exterius puros et simplices se ostendunt, interius duplicitatem celant; contra quos dicit Moyses: « Non induas vestem ex lana linoque contextam. » (Deut. XXII.)

VERS. 21.-- « Donec impleatur. » Non quod post, quod constat: sed non ante, quod dubium hominibus potest videri: quasi, nec ante judicium simplices deserit, nec malos percutere desinit.

« Impleatur risu os tuum, » id est mens gaudio 780D« aeternae securitatis, unde:

« Et labia tua jubilo. » Jubilum cum tanta laetitia corde concipitur, quam sermo non explicet, sed tamen os sonet.

VERS. 22.-- « Qui oderunt. » (Ibid.) Justorum hostes in judicio confusio induet, quia tunc viso judice mala omnia ante oculos mentis versantur, et reatus deprimens eos undique vestit. Qui ad hoc ideo veniunt, quia sola hic transitoria dilexerunt, unde addit:

« Et tabernaculum, » id est aedificatio aeternae felicitatis, quam supra se casuram multiplicant, ut se hic a necessatibus quasi ab aestu et imbre defendant: in qua mens habitat, quam bonus viator despicit.

CHAPTER VIII.

VERS. 1.-- « And answering. » (GREG.) It must be known first of all that, as Eliphaz noted the most holy Job chiefly for pride, though he touched briefly upon hypocrisy also, so this man too intends to note him chiefly for hypocrisy; but before he does this, 778Che sets something first, from which it is weighed how burdensome the words of Job himself had been to him, words namely of edification: as though therefore he could no longer endure a man speaking well, he broke out indignantly into these words, saying:

VERS. 2.-- « How long wilt thou speak such things. » As if to say: why dost thou speak useless things? and a manifold spirit . . . . .

« Still thou speakest such things. » (Ibid.) Because to a wicked man the words of edification are heavy, so that he cannot bear them, as it were; and because he does not wish to be corrected, he accuses things well said.

« And a manifold spirit. » (Ibid.) While he attributes manifoldness to his speech, he reproves the poverty of his understanding. And note that there are four kinds of speaking. 778DFor some are ample in mouth and in sense, who are to be praised; others straitened in both, who are to be pitied; others ample in sense, but unable to speak out, who are to be helped; others poor in sense, but torrential in tongue, who are to be despised: and this last kind this man twists into a charge against Job.

VERS. 3.-- « Doth God. » (GREG.) These things Job had neither denied nor been ignorant of, etc., as far as as though they had perished justly, they strike others, whence he adds:

VERS. 4, 5.-- « Even if thy sons have sinned, etc. If at daybreak: » as though they themselves were in the light, and the Church were in the night: which they summon, that it may rise through the knowledge of God, and through the prayer of penitence wash away things past, « if clean » in thought, « upright » in work.

779AVERS. 6.-- « He will awake. » He who now sleeps from giving help to thee who art erring.

« And he will render peaceable. » (GREG.) That is, he will restore the tranquillity of the present life, which alone they reckon to be God's reward.

« The dwelling of the just man. » (Ibid.) They say it is the counsel of the mind that is made peaceable, because if they draw men to their own side, they fall silent from contention. They promise also the riches of understanding by saying.

VERS. 7.-- « So much. » But because their words are not easily believed, they bend the sentences of the fathers into an argument for their own error, whence it is added:

« That thy former things. » Because they promise both that what was lost be recovered, and rewards of the present life greater still.

VERS. 8.-- « For ask the generations. » They advise that it be not seen, 779Bbut investigated, because they do not wish that to be looked upon which lies plainly open to all.

VERS. 9.-- « Of yesterday: » sometimes they teach morally that present things are to be inferred from things past, namely from their passing, that these are nothing; whence he adds, « of yesterday. » Because, if the former generation is called to mind, this life is known to pass swiftly as a shadow.

VERS. 10.-- « They shall teach. » They praise the fathers along with us, that thereby he may assail us the more.

« From the heart. » (Ibid.) As if to say: thou hast simplicity of spirit in the mouth, not in the heart: they the contrary. Often the wicked, not attending to themselves, tear the good; and either they bring forth good things which they have learned not by seeing but by hearing, or they thrust upon others the evils which they themselves do; and thus by their deeds they excuse themselves, by their words they judge. So Baldath, 779Ca feigner of justice, says wonderful things against hypocrites, and presumes to teach the just man about the things which would suit the depraved. Senseless is he who, while the gardens thirst, pours water into the river.

VERS. 11.-- « Can it live. » (Ibid.) He compares the hypocrite to a bulrush or a sedge, etc., as far as but let him show it by others' crimes.

« Before all herbs. » (Ibid.) The just also are herbs according to the flesh, because all flesh is grass (Isa. XLVIII); and they wither, because their works fail together with the life of the flesh. But those of the hypocrites wither before: because they forsake good things as soon as they have obtained the things for the sake of which they feigned. These are « the grass of the housetops, which withered before it was plucked up. »

VERS. 13.-- « So are the ways of all. » Behold to what he compares 779Dthe bulrush or the sedge: the hypocrite's hope is glory, and worldly things of this kind, which perish; eternal things abide.

VERS. 14.-- « His folly shall not please him. » (Ibid.) Folly is to sell a great thing at a cheap price, that is, to do laborious good works for favour: in this he is now delighted, but in punishment it will displease him, and then he will know that all the things which have passed were nothing, whence:

« And his confidence is like the spider's web. » The spider's web is woven with care, and is scattered by a breath of wind: so whatever the hypocrite sweats out, the breeze of favour carries off.

VERS. 15.-- « He shall lean upon his house. » The house of the mind is whatever thing the soul inhabits by love: the house of the hypocrite therefore is 780Afavour. Or, the house is confidence in one's own holiness, which will stand before men, but not before God.

VERS. 16.-- « He seemeth moist. » The bulrush, the hypocrite, seems moist in the night, that is, he appears to men as one filled with the grace of holiness, but before the sun, in the day of judgment, he will wither.

« And in his rising his shoot. » Every herb is first brought forth from the earth, etc., as far as the hypocrite, even when he acts rightly, wishes to be seen by many.

VERS. 17.-- « Upon a heap of stones. » (Ibid.) The roots are thoughts; the stones, because "living" is not added, are men both good and bad; the hypocrite therefore, who lingers among the stones, multiplies his thoughts in seeking the admiration of men: for through all that he does he seeks praises with hidden thought, having openly 780Bthat which his enemies may plunder. As Hezekiah, who after many benefits done to him showed all his goods to the messengers of the king of Babylon: whence he presently hears: « All shall be carried off into Babylon (II Reg. XX); » [Vulg. (IV Reg. XX, 17) auferentur omnia, "all things shall be carried away"] so too, while praise is sought, virtue and beauty are handed over into the hand of the enemy; whence Baldad says:

« And he shall abide among the stones. » Because he stands there, where he makes firm the intention of his mind: and though he be full of good things, if God shall have swallowed him up, that is, taken him away by death, from his place, that is, from the favours of this life; he will say: I know thee not, as it is said to the foolish virgins (Matth. XXV): and rightly, because it is not enough for him unless he has made others also like himself, and this is what he says: for this is the joy of his way.

« For this is the joy, etc. (Ibid.) That he may be punished also for their 780Cguilt, but:

VERS. 20.-- « God will not cast away the simple man. » Whose simplicity may now be called folly by the craftiness of the double-minded.

« Nor will he reach out his hand. » (Ibid.) So as to raise them from the depth in which they seek glory. The malignant are hypocrites, who do not even do good things well. Or, because outwardly they show themselves to their neighbour pure and simple, while inwardly they hide duplicity; against whom Moses says: « Thou shalt not put on a garment woven of wool and linen together. » (Deut. XXII.)

VERS. 21.-- « Until it be filled. » Not that afterward, which is certain: but not before, which may seem doubtful to men: as if to say, he neither forsakes the simple before the judgment, nor ceases to strike the wicked.

« Let thy mouth be filled with laughter, » that is, the mind with the joy 780D« of eternal security, whence:

« And thy lips with rejoicing. » Jubilation is conceived in the heart with such great gladness as speech may not unfold, and yet the mouth sounds it.

VERS. 22.-- « They that hate. » (Ibid.) Confusion shall clothe the enemies of the just in the judgment, because then, the judge being seen, all their evils are turned before the eyes of the mind, and the guilt that presses them down clothes them on every side. And they come to this for the reason that here they loved transitory things alone, whence he adds:

« And the tabernacle, » that is, the building of eternal felicity, which they multiply though it will fall upon them, that here they may defend themselves from necessities necessatibus as from heat and rain: in which the mind dwells, and which the good wayfarer despises.

CAPUT IX

  • 781A

VERS. 1-- « Et respondens Job ait. » (GREG. in Job. ) Perversi, quibus loquens persona displicet, omnia quae dicit, prava seu recta impugnant; sed Job cui non displicet persona, sed culpa, videns Baldath recta dixisse: « Nunquid Deus supplantat judicium » : et vera contra hypocritas protulisse, audita probat. Vere scio quod homo humilis et Deo suppositus justitiam accipit, compositus perdit, quia qui se ei comparat, quod Dei est sibi arrogans, bono quod accepit, se privat. Sed et omne meritum nostrae virtutis (si districte judicetur) vitium est.

VERS. 3.-- « Si voluerit contendere cum eo, » (Ibid.) de suis meritis praesumendo, et gloriam 781Bsuae virtutis, non Deo, sed sibi arrogando.

« Non poterit ei respondere unum pro mille. » (Ibid.) Id est non poterit se, etc., usque ad ne autem contendat, infirmum terreat visa ejus potentia.

VERS. 4.-- « Quis restitit ei et pacem habuit. Quis creat ut sibi creata conveniant, ordinat in quo sit eis pax, et qui ejus dispositioni repugnare conatur, pacem perdit, ut angelus, qui, sua inquietudine elatus, homo qui carnis molestia vexatur, quia conditori subesse noluit, pacem uterque amisit.

VERS. 5.-- « Qui transtulit montes, » etc. (Ibid.) Dicta generaliter confusione superbiae, quae poena maneat elatos in interitu Israel ostendit. Transtulit montes, sanctos praedicatores a Judaeis ad gentes, quod nescierunt Judaei, qui, in hoc subversi sunt, putantes 781Csibi gaudium quod lumen perdiderunt. Translatis montibus, commovet terras.

VERS. 6.-- « Commovet terram de loco. » Quia Judaeos de finibus suis per Romanos extraxit.

« Columnae. » Sacerdotes, principes, legis doctores. Concussi sunt, quia nec ipsi sibi vivere permissi sunt, unde apostolos expulerunt, praeterea in erroris sui tenebris deserti sunt.

VERS. 7.-- « Qui praecipit soli. » (Ibid.) Sol per diem lucet, etc., usque ad in omnes gentes dilatatur. Unde:

VERS. 8.-- « Qui extendit coelos. » (Ibid.) Id est, apostolos a Judaeis fugatos ubique facit honorari.

« Solus. » Qui mire hoc dispensavit, et cum amaritudo mundi in nece bonorum saeviret.

781D« Graditur super fluctus, » id est, calcat dum mitigat admiratione signorum. Jeremias: « Posui arenam terminum mari (Jer. 5), » id est, ad frangendam gloriam mundi, abjectos et pauperes elegit; dum autem mare saevit, Ecclesia proficit.

VERS. 9.-- « Qui facit arcturum. » (Ibid.) Nominibus philosophorum utitur, etc., usque ad imo ut arcturus versatur, erigitur.

« Oriona. » (Ibid.) Qui hiemali tempore oriuntur, etc., usque ad sed placidos in eorum necem erexit.

« Hyadas. » In fronte Tauri in vere nascuntur, etc., usque ad supernae patriae interna perveniat, unde addit:

« Et interiora austri. » Auster fervor Spiritus 782Asancti, etc., usque ad melius deficiendo comprehendit, dicens:

VERS. 10.-- « Qui facit mihi. » Melius explet, qui se non posse explere fatetur, et facundius loquitur qui obstupescendo tacet.

« Magna » virtute.

« Et incomprehensibilia » ratione.

« Et mirabilia quorum non » multiplicitate. Sed quid miremur quae extra nos sunt, cum et hoc quod erga nos agitur, ignoramus? Si venerit ad me.

VERS. 11.-- « Si venerit ad me. » (Ibid.) Venit, cum dona dat, etc., usque ad et tamen sic caecus homo rationem reddet de actibus Deo.

VERS. 12.-- « Si repente inter. » (Ibid.) Repente interrogat, cum inopinatos, etc., usque ad, quod planius 782Bostenditur dum subditur:

« Quis dicere potest, cur ita facis? » Facta Dei debemus venerari, etc., usque ad quam superbus inexstinguibiliter accendit, unde ait:

VERS. 13.-- « Deus cujus irae resistere nemo potest. » Moyses et Phinees et alii sancti, etc., usque ad ab his reversus ad se humiliter prae potestate Dei sibi vilescit.

VERS. 14.-- « Quantus ergo se. » (Ibid.) Id est, si angelus hunc considerare non sufficit, qua mente de ejus judiciis disputo qui carne premor? Verba nostra ad Deum sunt opera, sed his quia non potest homo loqui cum Deo, quia in eis apud subtile judicium Dei nulla fiducia est, unde addit:

VERS. 15.-- « Qui etiam si habuero, etc., deprecabor. » 782CPrece post justitiam Dei indiget, ut quae minus ex se, ex pietate judicis valeat. Etsi plena in perfectioribus sit justitia, haberi de illa,

« Quidpiam, » dicitur, quia mens hominis quae comprehendit vix peragit, et parum est quod comprehendit. Oratio vero, quia multis cogitationibus quatitur, etsi auditur, repelli timet.

VERS. 16.-- « Cum invocantem exaudierit me non. » (Ibid.) Cum enim (ut ait David) « modo ascendunt usque ad coelos (Psal. CVI), » cum summa penetrant, modo descendunt in abyssos, per turpia tentamenta, certitudinem exauditionis non habent. Vide quam subtiliter se judicat, infirmitatem suam aperit.

« Quantus ergo. » In justitia non confidit, sed ad 782Dprecem confugit: qui, etiamsi habuero quidpiam justum, de ipsa quoque prece pavidus ait: « Cum invocantem. » Ideo sic circumspicit, quia vim judicii se non posse ferre videt, de quo ait:

VERS. 17.-- « In turbine etiam conteret me. » Qui in tranquillitate exspectatus contempsit, in turbine conteretur.

« Turbine: » quia in commotione elementorum ibi districte requiri opera timet: cum et quosdam sine operibus originali reatu damnari videt, quorum voce ait:

« Multiplicabit vulnera mea. » Quia ipsi nihil egerunt, unde dicitur: « Non est mundus in conspectu ejus, nec infans unius diei. »

« Sine causa. » Pro aestimatione humani sensus; 783Ased apud Deum justum est, ut arbor in ramis amaritudinem servet, quam traxit a radice. Haec etiam spiritualiter de Job, qui flagella Dei in turbine, et metuens praevidit, et praevidens pertulit, unde ait: « Et multiplicabit vulnera mea sine causa, » quia non pro culpa percussus est, unde supra, « ut affligerem eum frustra. »

VERS. 18.-- « Non concedit. » (GREG.) Plena probatio: extra lacerant tormenta, intus castigant tentamenta, unde se impletum amaritudinibus dicit, sed mitigat vim doloris aequitas et potentia ferientis. Et hoc est:

VERS. 19.-- « Si fortitudo quaeritur. » Quasi: nemo audet, quia ille merita examinat, qui per alienum testimonium ea non explorat, sed tacitus 783Bconsiderator diu exspectat. Alius non potest mihi esse testis, quia nec ego ipse mihi.

VERS. 20.-- « Si justificare. » ( Ibid. ) Non sufficio esse testis innocentiae, nec scio quod eam habeam.

VERS. 21.-- « Si simplex fuero. » Qui scit bona quae agit, etc., usque ad hoc exsilium caecitatis aegre fert, unde subdit:

« Et taedebit me vitae meae. » Sed est consolatio hujus caliginis reducta animo justa potentia Dei: quae et iniquos punit, et justos transcendit.

VERS. 22.-- « Unum est quod locutus sum. » ( Ibid. ) Vel « innocens » consumitur, etc., usque ad nostram duplam carnis et animae destruat.

VERS. 23.-- « Si flagellat, occidat se. » ( Ibid. ) Ridere 783CDei est afflictioni, etc., usque ad quod solus carnis mortem pertulit, aperit.

VERS. 24.-- « Terra data. » Vel « tradita. » Terra caro, impius diabolus.

« Manus » ejus, occisores Christi, etc., usque ad quam Christus venerit.

VERS. 25.-- « Dies mei velociores fuerunt cursore. » ( Ibid. ) Cursoris est, secutura nuntiare: sic omnes electi ante adventum nuntiantes Christum cursores fuerunt; sed quia ante moriuntur, transire se velocius cursore dolent, ut non videant praesentem illum qui singulariter bonus dicitur; de quibus etiam addit

VERS. 26.-- « Quasi naves. » Qui poma portat 783Dodore fruitur, etc., usque ad per compassionem ad eorum duritiam quoque sermo vertitur ita.

VERS. 27.-- « Cum dixero: nequaquam. » ( Ibid. ) Israel ut prius noluit loqui, cum quem praedixit negavit: in quo facies ejus interior commutatur sordibus perfidiae ut non agnoscatur a Deo, unde restat torqueri doloribus aeternis.

« Dies mei velocius transierunt. » ( Ibid. ) Primus homo sic conditus est, ut vita ejus per tempora possit tendi, non evolvi; sed quia peccavit decursum pertulit, quem homo semper tolerat, et tamen optat, dum vivere appetit. Hujus decursus damna voce humani generis gemit.

« Fugerunt, » quia ad lucem stare homo noluit, 784Afugiendo visum perdidit, ne bonum quod est, videre possit, ad quod conditus fuit.

« Quasi naves poma portantes. » ( Ibid. ) Fructus terrae per fluctus, etc., usque ad quae cogitatio ne in superbiam erumpat, humiliter se comprimit dicens:

« Nequaquam ita loquar. » Sed infirmus a tali inquisitione compescar. In quo facies mentis commutatur, quia quae alta quaerebat, infirmam se videns, veneratur quod ignorat. In qua est dolor, quia valde affligitur qui ad intelligenda quae de se sunt, caecatur. His communibus malis sua propria adjungere metuit: « Verebar omnia, » etc.

VERS. 28.-- « Verebar omnia opera. » ( Ibid. ) Bona quidem erant, etc., usque ad quasi nec per labores ad vitam perveniat, unde:

784BVERS. 29.-- « Si autem et sic impius sum. » Non poenitet laborasse, sed inter labores de praemiis incertum se esse dolet. Ita est incertus justus, ut confidat, ita confidit ne securus torpeat.

« Frustra: » dico quia.

VERS. 30.-- « Si lotus fuero quasi aquis nivis. » Qui conterit se ut lamentis, etc., usque ad qui compungitur in prece pro terrenis, aqua terrae.

« Manus, » ( Ibid. ) sunt opera.

« Velut mundissimae, » quasi non vere mundae sub hac corruptione.

VERS. 31.-- « Intinges » id est, intinctum demonstrabis, quia quanto plus ei approximamus, sordes nostras ex ejus munditia agnoscimus.

« Vestimenta. » Vestimentum animae est corpus 784Cquod praecepit Salomon omni tempore candidum esse, et si sit mistum sanguine, id est carnalibus desideriis, dicit Isaias comburendum (Isa. 9).

« Abominabuntur, » id est, abominabilem reddent, ut de Juda dicitur: « Possedit agrum de mercede iniquitatis, » id est, possideri fecit. In abominatione vestis hoc gravius habet, quod sensum sui reprehensoris ignorat.

VERS. 32.-- « Neque enim viso. » Inter homines, et audimus objecta, et ad ea respondemus et audimur; sed in judicio Dei, ipse nos audit, quia videt omnia quae agimus, sed non audimus eum, quia quod ei displicet plene non intelligimus. Non ex aequo potest audiri.

VERS. 33.-- « Utrumque valeat: » id est Deum 784Det me possit:

« Arguere. » Arguit, qui ex auctoritate rationis, etc., usque ad quod corda delinquentium mansuetudine potius quam terrore correxit, unde addit:

VERS. 34.-- « Auferat a me virgam » legis qua punit; sed eam Christus abstulit, et per mansuetudinem vias vitae ostendit. Voluit ut Deus timeri, sed ut pater amari.

VERS. 35.-- « Loquar et non timebo eum. » ( Ibid. ) Jam non habet metum, sed ut ad patrem affectum. Ecce baculus, id est timor legis missus non suscitat puerum; sed ipse Elisaeus, puero contemperatus, et septies aspirans, id est per amorem spiritus.

785A(GREG.) « Respondere » est factis digna recompensare, sed beneficiis Dei nemo serviliter timens respondet? Tantummodo qui per fiduciam amoris non timet digna ei reddi obsequia. Non est purus, qui mala ageret, si liceret. Amanti autem est taedium hujus vitae, quae prius dulcis erat etiam moerens eam tolerat.

CHAPTER IX

  • 781A

VERS. 1-- « And Job answering said. » (GREG. in Job. ) The perverse, to whom the person speaking is displeasing, attack all that he says, whether crooked or right; but Job, to whom not the person but the fault is displeasing, seeing that Baldath had said right things: « Doth God supplant judgment » : and had brought forward true things against hypocrites, approves what he has heard. Truly I know that a man who is humble and set beneath God receives justice, and one set alongside him loses it, because he who compares himself with him, arrogating to himself what is God's, deprives himself of the good which he received. But also every merit of our virtue (if it be strictly judged) is a vice.

VERS. 3.-- « If he will contend with him, » (Ibid.) by presuming upon his own merits, and by arrogating the glory 781Bof his own virtue not to God but to himself.

« He shall not be able to answer him one for a thousand. » (Ibid.) That is, he shall not be able to, etc., as far as but lest he contend, let his power, once seen, terrify the weak man.

VERS. 4.-- « Who hath resisted him and had peace. Who creates, that the things created may accord with him, ordains wherein there may be peace for them; and he who strives to fight against his disposing loses peace: as the angel, who, lifted up by his own unquiet, and man, who is vexed by the trouble of the flesh, each of the two lost peace, because he would not be subject to his Creator.

VERS. 5.-- « Who hath removed mountains, » etc. (Ibid.) The confusion of pride having been spoken of in general, he shows in the destruction of Israel what punishment awaits the lifted up. He removed the mountains, that is, the holy preachers, from the Jews to the Gentiles, which the Jews did not know, who were overthrown in this, reckoning 781Cit a joy to themselves that they had lost the light. The mountains being removed, he moves the lands.

VERS. 6.-- « He moveth the earth out of its place. » Because he drew the Jews out of their own borders by the Romans.

« The pillars. » The priests, the princes, the doctors of the law. They were shaken, because not even they were allowed to live to themselves, whence they drove out the apostles; and besides they were left forsaken in the darkness of their own error.

VERS. 7.-- « Who commandeth the sun. » (Ibid.) The sun shines through the day, etc., as far as it is spread abroad into all nations. Whence:

VERS. 8.-- « Who spreadeth out the heavens. » (Ibid.) That is, he causes the apostles, put to flight by the Jews, to be honoured everywhere.

« Alone. » Who wonderfully dispensed this, and that while the bitterness of the world raged in the slaying of the good.

781D« He walketh upon the waves, » that is, he treads them down while he calms them by the wonder of signs. Jeremias: « I have set the sand a bound for the sea (Jer. 5), » that is, to break the glory of the world he chose the abject and the poor; and while the sea rages, the Church advances.

VERS. 9.-- « Who maketh Arcturus. » (Ibid.) He uses the names of the philosophers, etc., as far as nay rather, as Arcturus is turned about, he is raised up.

« Orion. » (Ibid.) Which rise in the wintry season, etc., as far as but he raised up the peaceable unto their slaying.

« The Hyades. » They are born in the forehead of Taurus in the spring, etc., as far as he may come through to the inward things of the country above, whence he adds:

« And the inner parts of the south. » The south wind is the fervour of the Holy 782ASpirit, etc., as far as he comprehends better by failing, saying:

VERS. 10.-- « Who doeth for me. » [Vulg. qui facit magna, "who doeth great things"] He fulfils it better who confesses that he cannot fulfil it, and he speaks more eloquently who is silent in astonishment.

« Great » in power.

« And incomprehensible » in reason.

« And wonderful things whereof there is not » in manifoldness. But why should we wonder at the things which are outside us, when we are ignorant even of what is done concerning us? If he come to me.

VERS. 11.-- « If he come to me. » (Ibid.) He comes when he gives gifts, etc., as far as and yet man, thus blind, shall render an account of his acts to God.

VERS. 12.-- « If suddenly he ask. » (Ibid.) He asks suddenly, when the unexpecting, etc., as far as, which is shown more plainly 782Bwhen it is added:

« Who can say, Why dost thou so? » We ought to venerate the deeds of God, etc., as far as which the proud man kindles inextinguishably, whence he says:

VERS. 13.-- « God, whose wrath no man can resist. » Moses and Phinees and other saints, etc., as far as returning from these to himself, he humbly grows vile in his own eyes before the power of God.

VERS. 14.-- « How great therefore himself. » (Ibid.) That is, if an angel does not suffice to consider him, with what mind do I, who am pressed by the flesh, dispute of his judgments? Our words to God are works, but with these; because man cannot speak with God, because in them there is no confidence before the subtle judgment of God; whence he adds:

VERS. 15.-- « Who, even if I shall have anything, etc., I will beseech. » 782CAfter God's justice he stands in need of prayer, that what avails less of itself may avail from the mercy of the judge. Though justice be full in the more perfect, « anything » is said to be had of it, because the mind of man scarcely accomplishes what it comprehends, and little is that which it comprehends. But prayer, because it is shaken by many thoughts, even if it is heard, fears to be repelled.

VERS. 16.-- « When he shall have heard me calling, not. » (Ibid.) For when (as David says) « now they mount up to the heavens (Psal. CVI), » when they penetrate the heights, and now they go down into the depths through shameful temptations, they have no certainty of being heard. See how subtly he judges himself, and lays open his own weakness.

« How great therefore. » He does not trust in justice, but flees to 782Dprayer: who, even if I shall have had anything just, being fearful about that very prayer, says: « When calling. » He looks about him thus for this reason, that he sees he cannot bear the force of the judgment, of which he says:

VERS. 17.-- « In a whirlwind also he will crush me. » He who, being awaited in tranquillity, despised, shall be crushed in a whirlwind.

« A whirlwind: » because in the commotion of the elements he fears that works are there strictly required: since he sees that some also are damned without works by original guilt, in whose voice he says:

« He will multiply my wounds. » Because they themselves did nothing, whence it is said: « There is none clean in his sight, nor an infant of one day. »

« Without cause. » According to the estimation of human sense; 783Abut with God it is just, that a tree should keep in its branches the bitterness which it drew from the root. These things also spiritually of Job, who both, fearing, foresaw the scourges of God in the whirlwind, and, foreseeing, bore them; whence he says: « And he will multiply my wounds without cause, » because he was not struck for a fault; whence above, « that I should afflict him in vain. »

VERS. 18.-- « He granteth not. » (GREG.) A full proving: without, torments tear; within, temptations chastise; whence he says that he is filled with bitternesses; but the equity and the power of him who strikes tempers the force of the sorrow. And this is:

VERS. 19.-- « If strength be sought. » As if to say: no one dares, because he examines merits who does not search them out by another's testimony, but as a silent 783Bobserver waits long. Another cannot be a witness for me, because not even I myself for myself.

VERS. 20.-- « If I would justify. » ( Ibid. ) I do not suffice to be a witness of innocence, nor do I know that I have it.

VERS. 21.-- « If I be simple. » He who knows the good things which he does, etc., as far as he bears ill this exile of blindness, whence he adds:

« And I shall be weary of my life. » But there is a consolation for this darkness: the just power of God brought back to the mind, which both punishes the wicked and surpasses the just.

VERS. 22.-- « One thing there is which I have spoken. » ( Ibid. ) Or « the innocent » is consumed, etc., as far as he may destroy our double of flesh and of soul.

VERS. 23.-- « If he scourge, let him kill himself. » [Vulg. occidat semel, "let him kill at once"] ( Ibid. ) For God to laugh 783Cis at affliction, etc., as far as he opens that he alone bore the death of the flesh.

VERS. 24.-- « The earth is given. » Or « delivered up. » The earth is the flesh, the wicked one the devil.

« The hands » of him, the slayers of Christ, etc., as far as than Christ came.

VERS. 25.-- « My days have been swifter than a courier. » ( Ibid. ) It belongs to a courier to announce what is to follow: so all the elect who before his coming announced Christ were couriers; but because they die beforehand, they grieve that they pass more swiftly than a courier, so that they do not see him present who is singularly called good; concerning whom also he adds

VERS. 26.-- « Like ships. » He who carries apples 783Denjoys their fragrance, etc., as far as through compassion the discourse is turned also to their hardness, thus.

VERS. 27.-- « When I shall say: By no means. » ( Ibid. ) Israel, as before, would not speak, when he denied him whom he had foretold: in which his inner face is changed with the filth of perfidy, so that he is not acknowledged by God; whence it remains for him to be tormented with eternal sorrows.

« My days have passed more swiftly. » ( Ibid. ) The first man was so made that his life could be stretched out through times, not run out; but because he sinned he underwent a running-down, which man always endures, and yet desires, so long as he seeks to live. The losses of this running-down he laments with the voice of the human race.

« They have fled, » because man would not stand in the light, 784Aby fleeing he lost his sight, so that he could not see the good which is, for which he was made.

« Like ships carrying apples. » ( Ibid. ) The fruits of the earth through the waves, etc., as far as lest that thought break out into pride, he humbly restrains himself, saying:

« I will by no means speak thus. » But, being weak, let me be restrained from such an inquiry. In which the face of the mind is changed, because that which sought high things, seeing itself weak, venerates what it does not know. In which there is sorrow, because he is greatly afflicted who is blinded for the understanding of the things which are of himself. To these common evils he fears to join his own: « I feared all, » etc.

VERS. 28.-- « I feared all my works. » ( Ibid. ) They were indeed good, etc., as far as as though not even through labours he might come to life, whence:

784BVERS. 29.-- « But if I am wicked even thus. » He does not repent of having laboured, but grieves that amid his labours he is uncertain about the rewards. So is the just man uncertain, that he may trust; so he trusts, lest being secure he grow torpid.

« In vain: » I say because.

VERS. 30.-- « If I be washed as it were with waters of snow. » He who crushes himself as with laments, etc., as far as he who is pricked in prayer for earthly things, the water of the earth.

« The hands, » ( Ibid. ) are works.

« As most clean, » as though not truly clean under this corruption.

VERS. 31.-- « Thou shalt dip » that is, thou shalt show me dipped, because the more we draw near to him, the more we recognize our own filth from his cleanness.

« The garments. » The garment of the soul is the body, 784Cwhich Solomon commanded to be white at all times; and if it be mingled with blood, that is, with carnal desires, Isaias says it must be burned (Isa. 9).

« They shall abhor, » that is, they shall render him abominable, as it is said of Judas: « He possessed a field of the reward of iniquity, » that is, he caused it to be possessed. In the abhorrence of the garment it has this the heavier, that it does not know the mind of him who reproves it.

VERS. 32.-- « For neither, he being seen. » [Vulg. viro, "to a man"] Among men we both hear the charges brought against us, and answer them, and are heard; but in the judgment of God, he himself hears us, because he sees all that we do, but we do not hear him, because we do not fully understand what displeases him. He cannot be heard on equal terms.

VERS. 33.-- « Who may avail for both: » that is, may be able for God 784Dand for me:

« To reprove. » He reproves who from the authority of reason, etc., as far as that he corrected the hearts of offenders by gentleness rather than by terror, whence he adds:

VERS. 34.-- « Let him take away the rod from me » of the law, by which he punishes; but Christ took it away, and through gentleness showed the ways of life. He willed to be feared as God, but to be loved as a father.

VERS. 35.-- « I will speak and I will not fear him. » ( Ibid. ) He has fear no longer, but rather affection as toward a father. Behold, the staff, that is, the fear of the law, when sent does not raise up the boy; but Eliseus himself, brought down to the boy's measure, and breathing seven times, that is, through the love of the spirit.

785A(GREG.) « To answer » is to render in recompense deeds that are worthy; but does no one who fears in servile fashion answer to the benefits of God? Only he who through the confidence of love does not fear that worthy service be rendered to him. He is not pure who would do evil things if it were allowed. But to one who loves there is a weariness of this life, which before was sweet, even mourning he endures it.

CAPUT X.

VERS. 1.-- « Taedet animam meam vitae meae. » ( Ibid. ) Cum autem haec vita vilescit amore coelestis, qui defendebat culpas, jam eas accusat. Et hoc est:

« Dimittam eloquium » quo utebar in defensione mea.

785B« Adversum me, » quia confitendo me accuso, non ut qui adhuc erubescit confiteri, quia non tanto desiderio mala deserit, sed quia aliquando aperte confitens non gemit, sed lugenda gaudens dicit, subdit:

« Loquar in amaritudine, » ut amaritudo puniat, quod lingua accusat. Ex poenitentia nascitur securitas, et confidentior interrogat, quomodo erga se agatur. Et hoc est:

VERS. 2.-- « Dicam Deo: Noli me condemnare, » quia poena mea exstinguit tua supplicia.

« Indica mihi cur me ita judices, » id est flagellas, utrum ad securitatem vel pro qua culpa specialiter; quia etsi justus, habet tamen aliqua mala: et ideo, petit ut Deus se sibi indicet, ut quod Deus in eo punit, 785Cipse quoque flendo puniat. Ideo causam quaero; scio enim quod non injuste.

« Cur me ita judices. » Duobus modis hic judicat. Vel poenam immittit, etc., usque ad quem et feriendo in innocentia custodit. Dicit ergo:

VERS. 3.-- « Nunquid bonum. » Interrogatio haec negationem exigit, etc., usque ad quia infirmum considerat.

« Opus manuum. » Quasi impie non perdis quem gratuito fecisti.

« Impiorum, » id est malignorum spirituum, qui cum ad vitam redire nequeunt, socios ad mortem quaerunt, quorum consilium fuit, ut Job rectum in prosperis, frangerent adversis; sed Deus supra vires 785Dnon sinit tentari.

VERS. 4.-- « Nunquid oculi carnei. » Oculi carnis non nisi aliqua facta in tempore agnoscunt, quia et cum tempore clauduntur, et humanus visus quodlibet opus sequitur, non praecurrit.

VERS. 5.-- « Dies. » Dies quoque et anni hominis differunt ab aeterno: cui nihil praeterit, nihil est futurum, sed omnia conspicit praesentia.

VERS. 6.-- « Ut quaeras iniquitatem meam. » Et ita scias quod nihil impie feci, quasi humiliter quaerat: cur in tempore per flagella probas, quem apud te perfecte et ante tempora scisti?

VERS. 7.-- « Cum sit nemo » ( Ibid. ) Quasi, quid restat tibi nisi parcere, cui nemo potest obviare? Quo enim nullus potest tuam vindictam retinere, eo 786Afacilius pietas debet parcere; ut autem placetur, quia nostrum opus non possumus, suum opus ei offeramus.

VERS. 8.-- « Manus tuae. » Quasi pensa misericorditer, ne pereat quod fecisti.

« Fecerunt. » Secundum animam.

« Totum in circuitu. » Secundum corpus, etc., usque ad cum de caeteris dixit tantum: fiat.

« Et sic repente. » Quasi quem tanta dignitate praeponis, cur tanta vilitate despicis? Sed cum hac dignitate, quae est per spiritum? est infirmitas per carnem, quae moveat pium judicem, unde dicit:

VERS. 9.-- « Memento, quaeso, » etc. Unde venialis est culpa, cum irremissibilis sit in angelo, qui robustus fuit. Lutum est, cum aqua terram infundit: 786Bita homo, cum anima rigat carnem, cujus mortem aperte subdit:

« Et in pulverem, » etc. Quasi: vide materiam et poenam, et culpae parce; quia qualitatem hominis conditi protulit, subdit ordinem propagationis, unde dicit:

VERS. 10.-- « Nonne sicut lac, » etc. Factus homo ut lutum, propagatus autem sicut lac mulgetur semine, ut caseus coagulatur in carne. Carne et pelle induitur, ossibus et nervis solidatur. Post creationem corporis, quod minus est, subditur mira aspiratio vivificationis.

« Nonne sicut lac, » dum mens per Spiritum Dei et vetustate trahitur sicut lac mulgetur, quia in quadam inchoatione formatur; sicut caseus coagulatur, 786Ccum pinguescens cogitationibus stringitur, ut jam non defluat desideriis, sed in uno amore solidetur. Sed contra haec rudimenta caro ex veteri usu murmurat.

VERS. 11.-- « Pelle et carnalibus. » Id est carnalium motuum obsidione mens vallatur; qui autem exterius laxat ad bella, intus roborat per ossa virtutum, ut humilis sit et fortis. Ideo autem Deus recte vivendi rectitudinem tribuit, quia praeteritis delictis parcit.

VERS. 12.-- « Vitam et misericordiam. » ( Ibid. ) Sed haec bona frustra dedit, nisi ipse custodiat, unde subdit:

« Et visitatio tua. » Eadem de interiori homine, 786Detc., usque ad dona Dei cogitat subtilius.

« Vitam et misericordiam. » ( Ibid. ) Vita datur cum malis mentibus, etc., usque ad postquam, de se dixit dona misericordiae generaliter, subdit:

VERS. 13.-- « Tamen scio, quia universorum memineris » quod dicitur, cur de me trepido, qui omnes gentes colligendas scio.

« Licet haec celes in corde. » ( Ibid. ) Quia adhuc non indicas aperto sermone. Certi de venia, solent prioris nequitiae memoria tangi, et illicita cogitatione nolentes pulsari, unde subdit:

VERS. 14.-- « Si peccavi. » Ad horam parcit Deus, etc., usque ad sed pulsati in solo auxilio Dei spem fortius figant.

787AVERS. 16.--Propter superbiam. » (GREG.) Leaena dum catulis escam inhianter quaerit, etc., usque ad si autem a sancto desiderio torpemus, exempla bene vigilantium proponit.

VERS. 17.-- « Multiplicas iram tuam adversum me, » id est, multiplicem ostendis, quia et si ille bonos flagellat, quid erit impiis? Sic multiplici ira praevisa, homo ad se redit, et prava punit.

VERS. 19.-- « Fuissem quasi non essem, de utero. » ( Ibid. ) Idem quod ibi dictum est: « sicut abortivum absconditum, » etc., vel, qui concepti non viderunt lumen.

VERS. 20.-- « Nunquid non paucitas dierum meorum. » ( Ibid. ) Non capiat me delectatio praesentis vitae, quia cito transit.

787B« Dimitte ergo me . . . dolorem meum. » ( Ibid. ) Id est flagella tempera, etc., usque ad quod maxime facit praevisio inferni, unde ait:

VERS. 21.-- « Antequam vadam. » Infernus terra dicitur, quia ibi captivi tenentur.

« Antequam vadam. » Non quod qui plangit vadat, sed quod vadat qui non plangit: ut si dicam debitori, solve priusquam te constringam.

« Non revertar. » ( Ibid. ) Quia damnatos ultra misericordia non liberat.

VERS. 22.-- « Miseriae et tenebrarum. » Quia foris dolor cruciat, quos caecitas mentis intus obscurat; vel, ibi nemo ultra ad lucem redit, sicut in ista: ubi etsi sit miseria per conversionem licet reverti ad lucem.

787C« Umbra mortis. » Mors est separatio a Deo; umbra obscuritas ejus; quia ignis poenalis cremationem habet, et non lucem, ut in utroque puniantur, qui in utroque peccaverunt.

« Nullus ordo. » ( Ibid. ) Non quod Deus non ordinet, usque ad qualitas rerum non servatur. Unde et addit:

« Sempiternus horror. » Hic timor est futuri mali, etc., usque ad ne venirent ad tormenta quos in poena videret qui Lazarum vidit.

CHAPTER X.

VERS. 1.-- « My soul is weary of my life. » ( Ibid. ) But when this life grows cheap through love of the heavenly, he who was defending his faults now accuses them. And this is:

« I will let go my speech » which I was using in my own defense.

785B« Against me, » because by confessing I accuse myself, not as one who still blushes to confess, because he does not forsake his evils with so great a desire; but because sometimes a man confessing openly does not groan, but says with gladness what ought to be lamented, he adds:

« I will speak in bitterness, » that bitterness may punish what the tongue accuses. Out of penitence security is born, and more confidently he asks how it is dealt with toward him. And this is:

VERS. 2.-- « I will say to God: Do not condemn me, » because my punishment quenches thy torments.

« Show me why thou judgest me thus, » that is, thou scourgest me, whether unto security or for what fault in particular; because even if he is just, he yet has some evils: and therefore he asks that God show himself to him, so that what God punishes in him, 785Che too may punish by weeping. Therefore I seek the cause; for I know that it is not unjustly.

« Why thou judgest me thus. » In two ways he judges here. Either he sends a punishment, etc., as far as whom also by striking he keeps in innocence. He says therefore:

VERS. 3.-- « Is it good. » This question demands a denial, etc., as far as because he considers the weak man.

« The work of the hands. » As if to say: thou dost not impiously destroy him whom thou hast made freely.

« Of the impious, » that is, of the malignant spirits, who, since they cannot return to life, seek companions unto death; whose counsel it was that they should break by adversities Job who was upright in prosperity; but God 785Ddoes not suffer a man to be tempted above his strength.

VERS. 4.-- « Are the eyes of flesh. » The eyes of the flesh know nothing but certain deeds done in time, because they too are closed together with time, and human sight follows after whatever work there is, it does not run before it.

VERS. 5.-- « Days. » The days too and the years of man differ from the eternal: to whom nothing is past, nothing is to come, but he beholds all things present.

VERS. 6.-- « That thou mayest seek my iniquity. » And so that thou mayest know that I have done nothing impiously, as though he were asking humbly: why dost thou prove by scourges in time him whom thou hast known with thee perfectly and before all time?

VERS. 7.-- « Whereas there is no one » ( Ibid. ) As if: what remains to thee but to spare him whom no one can withstand? For by how much none can hold back thy vengeance, by so much the more 786Aeasily ought mercy to spare; and that he may be appeased, since we cannot offer our own work, let us offer him his own work.

VERS. 8.-- « Thy hands. » As if: weigh it mercifully, lest what thou hast made perish.

« They have made. » According to the soul.

« Wholly round about. » According to the body, etc., as far as when of the rest he said only: fiat.

« And so suddenly. » As if: him whom thou settest before others with such dignity, why dost thou despise with such vileness? But together with this dignity, which is through the spirit? there is infirmity through the flesh, which may move a merciful judge, whence he says:

VERS. 9.-- « Remember, I beseech thee, » etc. Whence the fault is venial, whereas it is unforgivable in an angel, who was strong. It is clay, when water floods the earth: 786Bso is man, when the soul waters the flesh, whose death he plainly adds:

« And into dust, » etc. As if: see the matter and the punishment, and spare the fault; and because he had set forth the quality of man as created, he adds the order of propagation, whence he says:

VERS. 10.-- « Was it not as milk, » etc. Man was made as clay, but propagated as milk is milked out by seed, that he may be curdled as cheese into flesh. He is clothed with flesh and skin, made solid with bones and sinews. After the creation of the body, which is the lesser thing, there is added the wondrous breathing-in of quickening.

« Was it not as milk, » while the mind is drawn by the Spirit of God and by its oldness as milk is milked out, because it is formed in a kind of beginning; as cheese it is curdled, 786Cwhen, growing rich, it is pressed together by thoughts, so that it no longer flows away in desires, but is made solid in one love. But against these first rudiments the flesh murmurs out of its old habit.

VERS. 11.-- « With skin and carnal things. » That is, the mind is walled about by the siege of carnal motions; but he who outwardly loosens it for wars, inwardly strengthens it through the bones of the virtues, that it may be humble and strong. And God rightly bestows the uprightness of living rightly, because he spares past offenses.
